Use the proved Gibbs normalization and second moment for the probability and moment fields. Compact-support generator integrability supplies the test-integral fields. Finally \(\int L\varphi\,d\pi_U=0\) makes the time integral vanish, while the constant curve has zero change in \(\int\varphi\,d\rho_t\). These verify each field of the weak equation for the stationary candidate.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Weak\allowbreak{}Fokker\allowbreak{}Planck\allowbreak{}Unique}{WeakFokkerPlanckUnique}}


This proposition quantifies over two curves in the stated weak solution class with the same initial measure and requires their equality throughout \([0,T]\). It is a named analytic uniqueness obligation. Defining the proposition does not prove it, and no axiom or automatic instance makes it available.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{gibbs stationary of weak\allowbreak{}Fokker\allowbreak{}Planck\allowbreak{}Uniqueness}{gibbs stationary of weakFokkerPlanckUniqueness}}


Compare the supplied weak solution \(\rho\) with the constant Gibbs weak solution constructed above. Their time-zero measures agree by hypothesis. The explicit \texttt{hunique} premise therefore identifies them at every time. Applying this conditional bridge to a stochastic process still requires its law curve to satisfy the weak equation and requires this uniqueness premise.

\section{\texorpdfstring{The standalone strongly convex IPLA error bound}{The standalone strongly convex IPLA error bound}}
\label{proof-module-IPLA-Theorem-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{IPLA/\allowbreak{}Theorem.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


For the fixed observed-data slice \(U_y\), assume differentiability, joint \(m\)-strong convexity with \(m>0\), \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient, \(N>0\), and \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L)\). Initialization is square integrable, and innovations have the stated independent standard Gaussian coordinate laws and initialization independence. Let \(\theta_*\) maximize the actual latent marginal. The theorem applies to every pair of independent natural dimensions, including zero.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Combine lifted transport with proved equilibrium concentration}{Combine lifted transport with proved equilibrium concentration}}


Suppose the state has at least one coordinate. The lifted geometry gives \(Nm\)-strong convexity and \(NL\)-smoothness in dimension \(D=d_\theta+Nd_x\). The Gibbs theorem normalizes \(\Pi_N\), while square-integrable initialization and Gibbs moments imply finite initial transport. Apply the shared Gaussian ULA theorem at step \(h=\gamma/N\), using the supplied Gaussian hypotheses. The step restriction rescales exactly and gives

\[W_2(\rho_k,\Pi_N)\le(1-m\gamma)^kW_2(\mu_0,\Pi_N)+\frac{33}{20}\frac Lm\sqrt{d_x+d_\theta/N}\sqrt\gamma.\]

The ULA output-finiteness result and the proved law identity ensure this is finite actual transport, before any real conversion. The equilibrium concentration theorem gives \(\operatorname{RMS}_{\Pi_N}(p_\theta-\theta_*)\le\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\). For any coupling of \(\rho_k\) and \(\Pi_N\), Minkowski and \(\|p_\theta\|\le1\) imply the sampled parameter RMS is at most coupling RMS plus that equilibrium radius. Taking the coupling infimum and using finiteness yields

\[\left(\int\|p_\theta z-\theta_*\|^2d\rho_k\right)^{1/2}\le\sqrt{\frac{2d_\theta}{Nm}}+(1-m\gamma)^kW_2(\mu_0,\Pi_N)+\frac{33}{20}\frac Lm\sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}{N}}\sqrt\gamma.\]

Stationarity, finite transport and concentration are derived here through the shared and particle theorems; they are not additional theorem hypotheses.




The proof first constructs target probability and initial finite transport from the lifted strong-convexity assumptions and \(L^2\) initialization. Gaussian ULA then gives the state-space transport rate, with step \(\gamma/N\), modulus \(Nm\), score constant \(NL\), and dimension \(d_\theta+Nd_x\). The law adapter identifies its output with the actual supplied IPLA recursion and proves the output transport distance is finite.



The projection inequality gives \(R_{\theta_*}(\rho_k)\le W_2(\rho_k,\Pi_N)+R_{\theta_*}(\Pi_N)\); equilibrium concentration supplies the second term. The facts \texttt{houtput} and \texttt{hmoment} ensure finite values before projected RMS is rewritten as a square-root integral. Finally, the exact particle/\allowbreak{}time scaling simplifies the transport terms. Thus normalization, stationary reference and parameter concentration are derived inputs, while joint strong convexity, smoothness and the Gaussian innovation laws remain the stated assumptions.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{nonempty\_\allowbreak{}dimension\_\allowbreak{}bound}: For fixed observations \texttt{y}, write \texttt{U y (pair theta x) = -\allowbreak{}log p\_\allowbreak{}theta(x,y)}. The general-dimension counterpart of equation (34), with independent parameter dimension \texttt{dtheta} and latent dimension \texttt{dx}, for the actual Gaussian IPLA recursion and its defined Gibbs target. Every hypothesis is displayed here or is one of the Gaussian-law and independence fields of \texttt{Gaussian\allowbreak{}Innovations}, whose definition is included in the standalone file. Normalization, all finite transport moments, a stationary Langevin reference, and the equilibrium parameter concentration are derived in the proof.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Include every zero-\allowbreak{}dimensional case in the public theorem}{Include every zero-dimensional case in the public theorem}}


The public theorem splits on whether the state coordinate index type is nonempty. In the nonempty case, instantiate the preceding theorem directly, retaining arbitrary independent \(d_\theta,d_x\). If the index type is empty, its parameter part forces \(d_\theta=0\); since \(N>0\), a positive \(d_x\) would supply a latent coordinate, so \(d_x=0\) as well. Normalize the Gibbs target by the same strong-convexity theorem and apply the singleton-state bound, where every error, transport distance and dimension term vanishes. This completes the displayed estimate in all dimensions. Existence and uniqueness of a permissible \(\theta_*\) are established separately by \texttt{exists\allowbreak{}Unique\_\allowbreak{}marginal\allowbreak{}Maximizer}, so the maximizer binder does not demand an unproved existence assumption.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{theorem1}: Strongly convex IPLA for independent, arbitrary parameter and latent dimensions. All stationary-reference and concentration inputs are derived. The zero-dimensional cases are included without extra assumptions.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Sharp parameter concentration and the IPLA RMS bound}{Sharp parameter concentration and the IPLA RMS bound}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SharpIPLA-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Sharp\allowbreak{}IPLA.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


A differentiable \(m\)-strongly convex pair potential \(U\), \(m>0\), has a marginal potential whose \(N\)-replicate target is \(Nm\)-strongly convex. The sharp dilation theorem improves its parameter second moment to \(d_\theta/(Nm)\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Concentration of the actual marginal target}{Concentration of the actual marginal target}}


For a continuous \(a\)-strongly convex potential \(f\) with minimizer \(x_\star\), the new dilation argument proves


\[
\int\|x-x_\star\|^2\,\pi(dx)\le\frac d a,
\qquad\pi(dx)\propto e^{-f(x)}dx.
\]


The proof uses a sharp radial growth inequality and a change of variables under dilation, rather than assuming an integration-by-parts identity for a nonsmooth density. It therefore applies directly to the continuous strongly convex Gibbs potentials needed here.



The existing Prékopa argument preserves strong convexity when latent variables are integrated out. Raising the marginal density to the \(N\)th power multiplies that modulus by \(N\). Applying the sharp estimate to its actual marginal potential gives


\[
W_2(\Pi_{\lambda,N}^{\theta},\delta_{\theta_{\lambda,\star}})
\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}.
\]


This matches the constant in v3 equation (17) and Lemma A.14. The previous IPLA theorem's \(\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\) estimate would have been weaker; the new result proves the sharper constant rather than labeling that factor as an error in the paper.




The exact parameter marginal of the lifted Gibbs measure is \(M(\theta)^N\) after normalization. Its negative log potential is \(NF(\theta)\), and the earlier Prékopa argument proves that it is \(Nm\)-strongly convex with minimizer \(\theta_*\). Sharp Gibbs concentration in dimension \(d_\theta\) gives \(\int\|\theta-\theta_*\|^2d\Pi_N\le d_\theta/(Nm)\). The pushforward identity transfers this same moment to the lifted state law before a square root is taken.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{sharp\_\allowbreak{}marginal\allowbreak{}Target\_\allowbreak{}second\allowbreak{}Moment\_\allowbreak{}le}: Sharp equilibrium second moment of the actual powered marginal density.
\item \texttt{sharp\_\allowbreak{}gibbs\allowbreak{}Target\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}second\allowbreak{}Moment\_\allowbreak{}le}: The exact parameter-marginal identity transfers sharp concentration to the full lifted Gibbs measure.
\item \texttt{sharp\_\allowbreak{}gibbs\allowbreak{}Target\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}concentration}: The parameter RMS radius with the sharp dimension constant.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Combine sharp concentration with the actual Gaussian-\allowbreak{}chain law}{Combine sharp concentration with the actual Gaussian-chain law}}


For \(N>0\), \(\gamma>0\), \(\gamma\le2/(m+L)\), Gaussian independent innovations and \(L^2\) initialization, use the scaled ULA transport theorem and the nonexpansive parameter projection. The projected triangle inequality gives

\[\operatorname{RMS}_n\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_N)+\frac{33}{20}\frac Lm\sqrt{d_x+d_\theta/N}\sqrt\gamma.\]

The private lemma handles nonempty coordinate sets. The public theorem splits off the empty-coordinate case, where the parameter norm and its error vanish, so no artificial positive-dimension assumption remains.




For \(D=d_\theta+Nd_x\), the lifted state has score constant \(NL\), convexity \(Nm\) and step \(\gamma/N\). The shared Gaussian ULA theorem controls its \(W_2\) error, while the parameter projection has norm at most one. The projected RMS therefore is at most the sharp equilibrium scale \(\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}\) plus that transport error. The nonempty-dimensional case proves this combination directly; the final declaration splits off zero-dimensional cases so that no unspoken positive-dimension premise remains.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{sharp\_\allowbreak{}ipla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms}: A complete actual Gaussian IPLA parameter bound with sharp concentration. This theorem includes zero parameter/\allowbreak{}latent dimensions.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Smoothed-\allowbreak{}model MYIPLA laws and finite-\allowbreak{}time convergence}{Smoothed-model MYIPLA laws and finite-time convergence}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYIPLA-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}MYIPLA.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Let \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\), \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\), where \(g_1\) is differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex and \(g_2\) is convex and bounded below. The actual expanded proximal recursion is identified with Gaussian IPLA for \(V_\eta\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Construct the right convexity and smoothness constants}{Construct the right convexity and smoothness constants}}


The scalar potential is \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\). Its envelope is convex, so adding the two convex-combination inequalities preserves strong convexity \(m\). Differentiability of both terms proves \(\nabla V_\eta=\nabla g_1+\nabla g_2^\eta\); the triangle inequality then adds gradient constants. The nonnegative-real constant used by Lean equals \(L_1+\eta^{-1}\) when \(\eta>0\).




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{smoothed\allowbreak{}Potential}: The pair potential of MYIPLA, with only its nonsmooth part regularized.
\item \texttt{smoothing\allowbreak{}Lipschitz}: Smoothing adds the inverse smoothing scale to the smooth gradient bound.
\item \texttt{smoothed\_\allowbreak{}strong\allowbreak{}Convex}: Adding a convex envelope preserves the strong convexity of the smooth part.
\item \texttt{smoothed\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}: The gradient Lipschitz constants of the smooth part and envelope add.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Identify actual laws before using convergence}{Identify actual laws before using convergence}}


Define \(\rho_n=Q\circ Z_n^{-1}\) from the explicitly expanded chain. The pointwise chain identity gives equality of pushforward laws with IPLA for any differentiable envelope having the stated residual gradient. The IPLA recursion is an actual \(L^2\) random variable. Projection by the bounded parameter map and subtraction of a constant preserve \(L^2\), proving finite parameter error about every fixed center without any convergence assumption.




The random variables are first identified by the exact residual-envelope update equality. Their almost-everywhere representative equalities then imply equality of pushforward measures, so the later convergence theorem concerns the actual MYIPLA law. Finite second moments follow from a Lipschitz score acting on \(L^2\) initial data and \(L^2\) innovations; the parameter error about a fixed center remains in \(L^2\) after applying the continuous parameter projection and subtracting a constant. These moment facts justify the ordinary RMS integral in the public bound.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{myipla\allowbreak{}Laws}: The law of the expanded MYIPLA recursion, for arbitrary supplied maps.
\item \texttt{myipla\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}ipla\allowbreak{}Laws}: Pointwise equivalence identifies the actual MYIPLA law with the IPLA law.
\item \texttt{myipla\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}mem\allowbreak{}Lp}: Every projected MYIPLA error has a finite second moment whenever the initialization and innovations are in \texttt{L2}. This is derived from the actual chain, without Gaussianity, equilibrium, or an error bound assumption.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Apply sharp IPLA to a general residual envelope}{Apply sharp IPLA to a general residual envelope}}


The Moreau envelope is convex. Therefore adding it to the \(m\)-strongly convex function \(g_1\) preserves the constant \(m\). Adding their gradient-Lipschitz estimates gives \(L_\lambda=L_1+1/\lambda\). The replicated-pair identity \(\sum_i\|J_i v\|^2=N\|v\|^2\) then yields lifted strong convexity \(Nm\), lifted smoothness \(NL_\lambda\), and lifted dimension \(d_\theta+Nd_x\).



Applying the existing Gaussian ULA result at step \(\gamma/N\) produces the contraction \((1-m\gamma)^n\) and the discretization term


\[
\frac{33}{20}\frac{L_\lambda}{m}
 \sqrt{d_x+d_\theta/N}\sqrt\gamma.
\]


The step condition \(\gamma\le2/(m+L_\lambda)\) is the corresponding scaled ULA condition. Initial finite second moments and Gaussian-law independence are explicit. The continuous reference and finite transport quantities are constructed by the shared theorem, so no convergence estimate or invariant measure is an additional MYIPLA hypothesis.



The law-identification helpers also prove that the actual expanded recursion is probability-valued and that its parameter error about every fixed center belongs to \(L^2\). These guards are used before passing from extended norms to ordinary real square-root integrals in the original-center theorem.



With \(\theta_\eta\) a maximizer of the smoothed marginal and \(\Pi_{N,\eta}\) its actual Gibbs reference, the complete statement is

\[\left(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_\eta\|^2\right)^{1/2}\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\eta})+\frac{33}{20}\frac{L_\eta}{m}\sqrt{d_x+d_\theta/N}\sqrt\gamma.\]

The generic theorem allows any convex differentiable envelope with the stated residual gradient and \(\eta^{-1}\) gradient constant; the next step derives those properties for the true Moreau envelope.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{myipla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}smoothed}: An explicit finite-time RMS parameter bound for MYIPLA around the maximizer of its smoothed marginal likelihood. All Gaussian-law hypotheses describe the algorithm's actual innovations. The dependence on the smoothing scale is explicit through \texttt{L\ensuremath{{}_{1}} + eta-\allowbreak{}\ensuremath{{}^{1}}}.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Discharge the envelope and moment hypotheses}{Discharge the envelope and moment hypotheses}}


For the actual Moreau envelope, the proximal calculus proves all inputs: convexity, differentiability, \(\nabla g_2^\eta=\eta^{-1}(I-P_\eta)\), and gradient Lipschitz constant \(\eta^{-1}\). Substitute these into the generic smoothed theorem and the finite-moment lemma. The output is the pushforward by an almost everywhere measurable \(L^2\) representative; mapping the whole probability space to the whole state space proves total mass one.




For the genuine envelope, the proximal module supplies the residual identity \(\nabla g_2^\eta=(I-P_\eta)/\eta\), its differentiability and convexity, and the score Lipschitz constant \(\eta^{-1}\). Adding the smooth part gives \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\), modulus \(m\) and score constant \(L_1+\eta^{-1}\). The theorem therefore discharges each abstract envelope assumption before applying the sharp IPLA estimate. The associated probability and moment declarations establish that the final law has total mass one and a finite projected RMS, independently of merely writing its integral.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms}: MYIPLA with the actual proximity minimizer and its actual Moreau envelope. Every analytic envelope property used by the stochastic theorem is derived from convexity and a lower bound on \texttt{g\ensuremath{{}_{2}}}. In particular, no residual-gradient identity, stationarity result, concentration bound, or convergence estimate is an additional hypothesis of this theorem.
\item \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}mem\allowbreak{}Lp}: Finite moments for MYIPLA with its genuine Moreau/\allowbreak{}proximal construction.
\item \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Probability}: The actual MYIPLA output is a probability law. Measurability follows from the identified \texttt{L2} recursion, rather than being assumed for the map.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Move the center of a finite projected RMS error}{Move the center of a finite projected RMS error}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ProjectionCenter-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Projection\allowbreak{}Center.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


For a probability measure \(\mu\) and bounded linear map \(T\), write \(R_\mu(a)=\|T(\cdot)-a\|_{L^2(\mu)}\), initially as an extended nonnegative number. Finiteness is checked before converting this norm to a real number.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Minkowski and finiteness}{Minkowski and finiteness}}


The identity \(Tx-a=(Tx-b)+(b-a)\) and Minkowski yield \(R_\mu(a)\le R_\mu(b)+\|b-a\|\). A constant has that \(L^2\) norm because \(\mu\) has mass one. If \(R_\mu(b)<\infty\), the right side is finite, hence the error about \(a\) is finite too.




Write \(R_a=\|Tx-a\|_{L^2(\mu)}\). The code's \texttt{heq} is the pointwise identity \(Tx-a=(Tx-b)+(b-a)\). Since \(\mu\) has mass one, the constant function \(b-a\) has \(L^2\) norm exactly \(\|b-a\|\). Thus \texttt{e\allowbreak{}Lp\allowbreak{}Norm\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}le} gives \(R_a\le R_b+\|b-a\|\) even if \(R_b=\infty\). The second declaration uses the finite right-hand side to prove \(R_a<\infty\); it does not assume a second moment about the new center.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{projected\allowbreak{}RMS\_\allowbreak{}center\_\allowbreak{}le}: Minkowski controls the change of a parameter center, with infinite moments still represented by the extended norm.
\item \texttt{projected\allowbreak{}RMS\_\allowbreak{}center\_\allowbreak{}finite}: A finite error about one center implies finiteness about every center.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Pass to ordinary square-\allowbreak{}root integrals}{Pass to ordinary square-root integrals}}


Monotonicity of the extended-to-real conversion is valid once the upper bound is finite. This proves the real RMS inequality. Use the proved finite moments at both centers to identify the norms with \(\sqrt{\int\|Tx-a\|^2d\mu}\) and \(\sqrt{\int\|Tx-b\|^2d\mu}\). The resulting formula cannot hide an infinite moment through the totalized real integral.




The order of operations matters: first prove finiteness at \(b\), then at \(a\), then apply \texttt{ENNReal.\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}Real\_\allowbreak{}mono}. Only after these guards does \texttt{projected\allowbreak{}RMSReal\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}sqrt\_\allowbreak{}integral} identify both real norms with \((\int\|Tx-a\|^2d\mu)^{1/2}\) and \((\int\|Tx-b\|^2d\mu)^{1/2}\). This prevents an infinite moment from being silently converted to the real number zero.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{projected\allowbreak{}RMSReal\_\allowbreak{}center\_\allowbreak{}le}: The usual real-valued center-change inequality, guarded by a proved finite moment at the comparison center.
\item \texttt{sqrt\_\allowbreak{}integral\_\allowbreak{}center\_\allowbreak{}le}: The integral form follows after both finite quadratic moments have been established.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{MYIPLA about the original nonsmooth marginal maximizer}{MYIPLA about the original nonsmooth marginal maximizer}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-Theorem-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Theorem.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Assume \(g_1\) is differentiable, \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient, and \(g_2\) is convex, bounded below and \(K\)-Lipschitz. Let \(m,\eta,\gamma>0\), \(N>0\), and \(\gamma\le2/(m+L_1+\eta^{-1})\). Initialization is in \(L^2\), and the actual Gaussian innovations are independent across times and coordinates and independent of initialization.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A finite approximation creates an actual original center}{A finite approximation creates an actual original center}}


The envelope gap gives \(V_\eta\le U\le V_\eta+\eta K^2/2\), where \(U=g_1+g_2\). The smooth strongly convex comparison supplies Gaussian domination and decay of the original marginal \(k_U(\theta)=\int e^{-U(\theta,x)}dx\). Its continuity and vanishing at infinity imply that it attains a positive maximum. Existence of this original center is derived, rather than added as an independent model premise.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{smoothed\allowbreak{}Potential\_\allowbreak{}gap}: The true smoothed pair potential is a one-sided uniform approximation of the original pair potential, with the sharp Lipschitz Moreau value gap.
\item \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}original\_\allowbreak{}maximizer\_\allowbreak{}exists}: The original nonsmooth model has an actual marginal maximizer under the primitive MYIPLA assumptions; no maximizer existence assumption is hidden.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Compare the actual marginal centers}{Compare the actual marginal centers}}


For the actual split Moreau approximation, \(\delta=\lambda K^2/2\), so


\[
\|\theta_\star-\theta_{\lambda,\star}\|
\le\sqrt{\frac{\lambda K^2}{m}}.
\]


The original and smoothed marginal maximizers exist under the primitive assumptions. The square-integrable triangle inequality then gives


\[
\left(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_\star\|^2\right)^{1/2}
\le\left(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_{\lambda,\star}\|^2\right)^{1/2}
 +\|\theta_{\lambda,\star}-\theta_\star\|.
\]


The finite-moment guards are proved before identifying extended norms with real square-root integrals. Substituting the smoothed-model convergence estimate proves the original-center result in Chapter 1.



This is deliberately weaker in smoothing order than Proposition A.1's claimed linear expansion. The formal theorem requires no Hessian along a parameter fiber and no uniform Taylor domination. The weaker order is a change in the formalized result, not a silently omitted term.




The finite envelope gap is \(0\le U-V_\eta\le\delta=\eta K^2/2\). Integrating the corresponding exponential inequalities in the latent variable and taking logarithms gives a parameter-marginal potential gap of the same size. If \(\theta_\eta\) minimizes the smoothed marginal potential, strong convexity yields \[\frac m2\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|^2\le\delta,\qquad\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|\le\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}.\] The hypotheses \texttt{hmax\allowbreak{}U} and \texttt{hmax\ensuremath{\eta}} identify the actual two marginal maximizers, so this is a bound on their displacement rather than the choice of an arbitrary comparison center.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}marginal\_\allowbreak{}center\_\allowbreak{}distance}: A genuine finite smoothing-bias bound for marginal maximizers. It holds without twice differentiability, a fibrewise Hessian premise, or Taylor remainders: squared displacement is at most \texttt{eta*K\ensuremath{{}^{2}}/\allowbreak{}m}.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Move the finite error and substitute the proved bound}{Move the finite error and substitute the proved bound}}


Let \(U=g_1+g_2\) on \(\mathbb R^{d_\theta+d_x}\). Assume \(g_1\) is differentiable, \(m\)-strongly convex with \(m>0\), and has \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient. Assume \(g_2\) is finite-valued, convex, bounded below, and \(K\)-Lipschitz. For \(\lambda>0\), set


\[
V_\lambda=g_1+g_2^\lambda,\qquad L_\lambda=L_1+\lambda^{-1}.
\]


Take \(N>0\), an initial state with finite second moment, independent standard Gaussian innovations at every coordinate and time, independent of initialization, and


\[
0<\gamma\le\frac{2}{m+L_\lambda}.
\]


Write \(\mu_n\) for the law of the actual scaled MYIPLA state and \(\Pi_{\lambda,N}\) for its continuous Gibbs target. Let \(\theta_\star\) maximize the original latent marginal \(\int e^{-U(\theta,x)}dx\). The formal estimate is


\[
\begin{aligned}
\left(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_\star\|^2\right)^{1/2}
\le{}&\sqrt{\frac{\lambda K^2}{m}}
 +\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}\\
&+(1-m\gamma)^n W_2(\mu_0,\Pi_{\lambda,N})\\
&+\frac{33}{20}\frac{L_\lambda}{m}
 \sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}{N}}\sqrt\gamma.
\end{aligned}
\]


The target normalization, finite transport moments, stationary reference, and equilibrium concentration are derived through the existing IPLA/\allowbreak{}ULA development. Neither the displayed convergence bound nor the smoothing bias is an extra assumption. The last term retains its explicit dependence on \(\lambda\); no uniformity in the smoothing scale is asserted. The paper's \(O(\lambda)+O(\lambda^2)\) smoothing expression is replaced by a finite \(O(\sqrt\lambda)\) guarantee, so this is a related proved theorem, not a verbatim reproduction of Theorem 4.1.



In Lean the proof first establishes that the actual MYIPLA law is probability-valued and its error about the smoothed center is square integrable. The center-change inequality then has valid finite real square-root integrals. Add the proved center displacement to the smoothed theorem, giving exactly the displayed four terms.




First apply the proved MYIPLA bound about \(\theta_\eta\). Its finite second moment makes the center-change inequality valid in real RMS form. Then \[\bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_U\|^2\bigr)^{1/2}\le\bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_\eta\|^2\bigr)^{1/2}+\|\theta_\eta-\theta_U\|.\] Substitute the preceding center displacement estimate. Thus smoothing bias, equilibrium concentration and finite-step algorithmic error enter for three different proved reasons; the original nonsmooth target is never assumed to have a Lipschitz score.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}original}: A fully proved finite-time theorem for the actual MYIPLA iterates and the original nonsmooth model's marginal maximizer. This is a finite replacement for the Taylor-based statement in v3 Theorem 4.1: the smoothing bias is \texttt{sqrt (eta*K\ensuremath{{}^{2}}/\allowbreak{}m)}, and the discretization constant explicitly depends on \texttt{eta}. There are no interpolation, entropy-production, or convergence hypotheses.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{The nonsmooth single-\allowbreak{}pair energy estimate}{The nonsmooth single-pair energy estimate}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ProximalEnergy-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal\allowbreak{}Energy.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


For convex \(f\), a true proximal minimizer \(p=P_\eta(x)\), positive \(\eta\), and a genuine input subgradient \(s\in\partial f(x)\), proximity controls both squared distance and the original nonsmooth energy.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Expand supports and complete a square}{Expand supports and complete a square}}


Let \(p=P_\lambda(y)\), \(s\in\partial g_2(y)\), and \(a\) be any comparator. Proximal optimality supplies the residual subgradient at \(p\). Combine its support inequality at \(a\) with the support inequality for \(s\) at \(p\), expand the squared distances, and use the nonnegative square \(\|(y-p)-\lambda s\|^2\). The result is v3 Lemma A.8:


\[
\|p-a\|^2\le\|y-a\|^2
 -2\lambda\bigl(g_2(y)-g_2(a)\bigr)+\lambda^2\|s\|^2.
\]


If \(\|s\|^2\le C\), replace the last term by \(\lambda^2C\). The proof works with any genuine subgradient satisfying that bound; it does not assume an energy estimate or a convergence statement. Taking the minimal section in C1 is one way to supply such a vector.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le}: Proximity decreases squared distance and nonsmooth energy, up to the squared norm of any subgradient at the input point.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Instantiate the chosen point and bound the forcing}{Instantiate the chosen point and bound the forcing}}


The existence theorem proves that the chosen \(P_\eta(x)\) meets the variational premise. Substitution gives \(\|P_\eta(x)-a\|^2\le\|x-a\|^2-2\eta(f(x)-f(a))+\eta^2\|s\|^2\). If \(\|s\|^2\le C\), multiply by the nonnegative \(\eta^2\) to replace the final term by \(\eta^2 C\). The vector must satisfy the true support inequality; a merely named subgradient is insufficient.




Expand \(\|p-y\|^2=\|x-y\|^2-2\langle x-p,x-y\rangle+\|x-p\|^2\), and use the residual subgradient at \(p\) to replace its pairing with the potential difference. A supplied subgradient \(s_x\in\partial f(x)\) bounds the remaining difference \(f(x)-f(p)\) by \(\langle s_x,x-p\rangle\). Completing the square gives \(2\lambda\langle s_x,x-p\rangle-\|x-p\|^2\le\lambda^2\|s_x\|^2\). Thus a bound \(\|s_x\|^2\le C\) produces the forcing \(\lambda^2C\). This algebra is why that forcing has a squared proximal scale.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le}: The deterministic nonsmooth energy estimate for the actual chosen minimizer of the proximal objective.
\item \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}subgradient\_\allowbreak{}bound}: A squared-subgradient bound supplies the forcing term in Lemma A.8.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Jensen repairs the averaged pairwise energy}{Jensen repairs the averaged pairwise energy}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ParticleEnergy-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Particle\allowbreak{}Energy.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


The scaled state is \(z=(\theta,x_1/\sqrt N,\ldots,x_N/\sqrt N)\), with \(N>0\), and \(J_i z=(\theta,x_i)\). The actual algorithm averages proximal parameter outputs; this module proves its energy inequality directly.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Averaging squares in the shared parameter}{Averaging squares in the shared parameter}}


Cauchy–Schwarz for finite sums gives \(N(N^{-1}\sum_i b_i)^2\le\sum_i b_i^2\). Apply it separately to each parameter coordinate of the pairwise outputs. Each latent block instead satisfies an exact equality after multiplying by \(N\), since \((\sqrt N)^2=N\). Summing all coordinates yields

\[\|\operatorname{Pair}_P(z)-a\|^2\le\frac1N\sum_i\|P(J_i z)-J_i a\|^2.\]


The first declaration is the scalar Jensen inequality \(N\bar b^2\le\sum_i b_i^2\), with \(\bar b=N^{-1}\sum_i b_i\). It follows by multiplying the average-of-squares inequality by positive \(N\); \texttt{hn} supplies exactly that sign and the nonzero denominator.



For the state-space comparison, write \(p_i=P(A_i z)\), with \(A_i z=(\theta,\sqrt N z_i)\), and let the comparison state be \(a\). In parameter coordinate \(j\), the discrepancy is the average of \(p_{i,\theta,j}-a_{\theta,j}\), since the common comparison parameter is the same for every pair. The local identity \texttt{hid} inside \texttt{h\ensuremath{\theta}} moves that constant through the average, and scalar Jensen gives

\[N\left|\frac1N\sum_i p_{i,\theta,j}-a_{\theta,j}\right|^2\le\sum_i|p_{i,\theta,j}-a_{\theta,j}|^2.\]

For particle coordinate \((i,j)\), unscaling instead gives the exact identity

\[N|N^{-1/2}p_{i,x,j}-a_{i,j}|^2=|p_{i,x,j}-\sqrt N a_{i,j}|^2.\]

This is \texttt{hx}, using \((\sqrt N)^2=N\). Expand the two Euclidean squared norms into finite coordinate sums, exchange the parameter and particle summations, and add the parameter inequalities to the exact latent identities. The result is \(N\|\operatorname{pairwiseMap}P(z)-a\|^2\le\sum_i\|p_i-A_i a\|^2\); division by \(N>0\) proves the displayed bound. No convexity or proximal property of \(P\) is used at this geometric stage.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{average\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}le}: Jensen's inequality for an average of real coordinates.
\item \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}dist\_\allowbreak{}le}: Squared distance of the actual averaged output is bounded by the average of the separate pairwise squared distances. This holds for every joint map.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Sum genuine proximal energy inequalities}{Sum genuine proximal energy inequalities}}


The roadmap on v3 page 19 identifies averaged pairwise proximity with the proximal map of the lifted averaged potential. Shared parameter coordinates prevent that identity in general. The needed energy conclusion can nevertheless be proved without changing the algorithm.



For arbitrary pair outputs, Jensen's inequality for their averaged parameter gives


\[
\|\operatorname{Pair}_P(z)-a\|^2
\le\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N\|P(J_i z)-J_i a\|^2.
\]


The scaled latent-coordinate contribution is an exact identity; only the parameter contribution requires an inequality. Apply the single-pair proximal energy result to every summand. Using \(\sum_i\|J_i(z-a)\|^2=N\|z-a\|^2\) and \(G_2=N^{-1}\sum_i g_2\circ J_i\) gives


\[
\|\operatorname{Pair}_{P_\lambda}(z)-a\|^2
\le\|z-a\|^2-2\lambda\bigl(G_2(z)-G_2(a)\bigr)+\lambda^2C.
\]


This lifted energy inequality is fully proved for actual minimizers. It repairs the deterministic proof bridge needed in Corollary A.13 while preserving the paper's particle algorithm and its original nonsmooth energy. It is still only one part of the full stochastic convergence proof.



The failure of the lifted-proximal identity has a concrete Lipschitz witness. Take \(N=2\), \(g_2(\theta,x)=|\theta-x|\), smoothing one, common input parameter \(1\) and physical particles \((0,4)\). The genuine pairwise proximal points are \((1/2,1/2)\) and \((2,3)\). Averaging only their parameter components gives \((5/4,1/2,3)\). In the lifted proximal objective with its scaled particle norm, the feasible point \((1,1,3)\) has value \(3/2\), while this pairwise output has value \(51/32\). The strict inequality is kernel checked below. The averaged energy proof above repairs the necessary estimate without this false identity.




Apply the preceding true proximal energy inequality separately at \(A_i z\), comparing to \(A_i y\). Sum all \(N\) inequalities, and use \(\sum_i\|A_i(z-y)\|^2=N\|z-y\|^2\). Jensen's inequality bounds the squared norm of the averaged parameter output, while the scaled latent output gives exact squared terms. After division by positive \(N\), the energy difference is \(N^{-1}\sum_i[f(A_i z)-f(A_i y)]\), and the remainder is the average of the actual squared subgradient bounds. This establishes the inequality for the implemented pairwise map without identifying it with a single proximal map on the shared state.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pairwise\_\allowbreak{}prox\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le}: The actual averaged pairwise proximal update satisfies the lifted energy estimate for the original nonsmooth potential. This proves the bridge needed in v3 Corollary A.13 directly, without a false \texttt{prox}-of-a-sum identity. The bound only needs one bounded subgradient at each pair input. In particular the paper's bound on the least-norm subgradient supplies this hypothesis whenever that minimal section is available.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Reference drift moments uniform over smoothing scales}{Reference drift moments uniform over smoothing scales}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingMoments-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Moments.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


For \(F=f+e\), assume \(F\) is \(m\)-strongly convex, \(f,e\) differentiable, \(\nabla f\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz, and \(\|\nabla e\|\le K\). The goal is a Gibbs drift bound using \(L,m,K\), without an inverse smoothing scale.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The envelope preserves a global value Lipschitz constant}{The envelope preserves a global value Lipschitz constant}}


Let \(p=P_\lambda(y)\). As a competitor at \(x\), use \(p+x-y\), so its quadratic penalty equals that of \(p\) at \(y\). The \(K\)-Lipschitz inequality for \(f\) changes the function value by at most \(K\|x-y\|\). Thus \(f^\lambda(x)\le f^\lambda(y)+K\|x-y\|\), and reversing \(x,y\) proves the same global Lipschitz constant.




Choose the actual minimizer \(p=P_\lambda(y)\), and test the objective at \(x\) with \(p+(x-y)\). The two quadratic penalties agree because \(p+(x-y)-x=p-y\); only the potential value changes, by at most \(K\|x-y\|\). Therefore \(f^\lambda(x)\le f^\lambda(y)+K\|x-y\|\). Reversing the inputs gives the absolute-value bound. This competitor argument proves a value Lipschitz bound uniform in \(\lambda\), rather than using the score's larger constant \(1/\lambda\).




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}: A Lipschitz potential has a Moreau envelope with the same Lipschitz constant.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Find a true minimum and bound the smooth force there}{Find a true minimum and bound the smooth force there}}


Strong convexity gives quadratic coercivity. Minimize \(F\) on a sufficiently large closed ball, with radius \(2\|\nabla F(0)\|/m+1\); the quadratic lower bound proves the resulting point minimizes globally. At a global minimum \(z\), the local-extremum derivative vanishes. Hence \(\nabla f(z)=-\nabla e(z)\) and \(\|\nabla f(z)\|\le K\).




The strong-convexity lower bound forces the total potential above its value at zero outside a finite ball. A minimizer on the compact ball is therefore global; this is the radius construction in \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}strong\allowbreak{}Convex\_\allowbreak{}minimum}. At a global minimizer of \(F=f+e\), the derivative vanishes, giving \(\nabla f(z)=-\nabla e(z)\). Thus a pointwise bound \(\|\nabla e\|\le K\) yields \(\|\nabla f(z)\|\le K\). This is the only value of the smooth score at a specific point needed in the moment estimate.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}zero\_\allowbreak{}at\_\allowbreak{}minimum}: A differentiable function has zero gradient at an actual global minimizer.
\item \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}strong\allowbreak{}Convex\_\allowbreak{}minimum}: A smooth strongly convex real-valued function attains its minimum.
\item \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}smooth\allowbreak{}Part\_\allowbreak{}at\_\allowbreak{}minimum}: At a minimum of the sum, a bounded second gradient bounds the first gradient.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A bounded gradient remains bounded in L\ensuremath{{}^{2}}}{A bounded gradient remains bounded in L²}}


The norm of the derivative of a \(K\)-Lipschitz scalar function is at most \(K\); the gradient is its Riesz representative and has the same norm. For a pointwise bounded drift \(\|b(x)\|\le K\), almost everywhere representative equality gives \(\|b(Y)\|_{L^2}\le K\) on a probability space.




The derivative of a globally \(K\)-Lipschitz scalar function has operator norm at most \(K\), and the Riesz identification preserves this norm, giving \(\|\nabla f(x)\|\le K\). Separately, if \(\|b(x)\|\le K\) for every input, then the representative equality for \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Drift} gives \(\|b(Y(\omega))\|\le K\) almost everywhere. Since \(P\) has mass one, its \(L^2\) norm is at most \(K\). This explains why a state-space uniform bound transfers without a dimension or measure-mass factor.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}: Global Lipschitzness bounds the actual gradient norm.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Drift\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}bounded}: A pointwise bounded Lipschitz drift is bounded by the same constant in \texttt{L2}.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Integrate a sharp quadratic bound about the mode}{Integrate a sharp quadratic bound about the mode}}


Lipschitz continuity and the mode bound give \(\|\nabla f(x)\|\le L\|x-z\|+K\), so \(\|\nabla f(x)\|^2\le2L^2\|x-z\|^2+2K^2\). Sharp Gibbs concentration supplies \(\mathbb E_\pi\|x-z\|^2\le d/m\), yielding

\[\mathbb E_\pi\|\nabla f\|^2\le2L^2d/m+2K^2.\]

For any \(Y\) with actual Gibbs law \(\pi\), the pushforward integral formula transfers this estimate and taking square roots gives \(\|\nabla f(Y)\|_2\le\sqrt{2L^2d/m+2K^2}\).




Let \(z\) minimize \(F=f+e\), with \(F\) \(m\)-strongly convex. From the previous stage, \(\|\nabla f(z)\|\le K\), and score Lipschitzness gives \(\|\nabla f(x)\|\le L\|x-z\|+K\). Consequently \[\|\nabla f(x)\|^2\le2L^2\|x-z\|^2+2K^2.\] The Gibbs second moment is integrable by strong convexity; the right side therefore supplies an integrable majorant for the score square. The facts \texttt{hbound} and \texttt{hgrad} discharge that prerequisite before \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}mono} is applied.



Sharp concentration yields \(\mathbb E_\pi\|X-z\|^2\le d/m\), so \(\mathbb E_\pi\|\nabla f(X)\|^2\le2L^2d/m+2K^2\). The next declaration transfers this actual Gibbs-law integral to any random variable with that law and takes its square root, obtaining the \(L^2\) drift bound used in the splitting reference.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}smooth\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}le}: A smoothing-uniform second moment for the smooth part of a Gibbs drift. Only the smooth part's own Lipschitz constant enters the bound.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Drift\_\allowbreak{}smooth\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le}: The corresponding actual \texttt{L2} smooth-drift bound for a random variable with the proved reference Gibbs law.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Lipschitz energy and the averaged lifted potential}{Lipschitz energy and the averaged lifted potential}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCoupling-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Coupling.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Let \(g\) be convex, bounded below and \(K\)-Lipschitz, \(\lambda>0\), and \(N>0\). The module proves energy estimates for the true pairwise proximal map and a value-Lipschitz bound for \(G=N^{-1}\sum_i g(J_i z)\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Obtain energy from value Lipschitzness}{Obtain energy from value Lipschitzness}}


At \(p=P_\lambda(x)\), the residual support at a comparator \(a\) controls \(f(p)-f(a)\). Bound \(f(x)-f(p)\le K\|x-p\|\), expand \(\|x-a\|^2\), and use \((\|x-p\|-\lambda K)^2\ge0\). Thus \(\|p-a\|^2\le\|x-a\|^2-2\lambda(f(x)-f(a))+\lambda^2K^2\). Sum over pairs, use Jensen for the shared parameter, and divide by \(N\) to get the exact averaged energy inequality with remainder \(\lambda^2K^2\).




The pointwise input is a genuine proximal minimizer \(p\). Its residual \(r=(x-p)/\lambda\) is a subgradient at \(p\) and has norm at most \(K\) when the potential is \(K\)-Lipschitz. Combining the support at \(p\), \(f(x)-f(p)\le K\|x-p\|\), and the quadratic expansion yields \[\|p-y\|^2\le\|x-y\|^2-2\lambda(f(x)-f(y))+\lambda^2K^2.\] Sum this for the original pairs and use parameter averaging to pass to the scaled state. The input is value Lipschitzness; no bounded subgradient selection or unproved lifted-proximal identity is hidden in the estimate.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}: The proximal energy inequality follows directly from a Lipschitz potential.
\item \texttt{pairwise\_\allowbreak{}prox\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}: The nonsmooth energy bound for the actual averaged pairwise proximal map, with a global Lipschitz hypothesis instead of an assumed subgradient selection.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Averaged pair distances control the physical-\allowbreak{}time potential}{Averaged pair distances control the physical-time potential}}


Define \(G(z)=F_g(z)/N\). Jensen and \(\sum_i\|J_i v\|^2=N\|v\|^2\) imply \(N^{-1}\sum_i\|J_i v\|\le\|v\|\). Therefore

\[|G(z)-G(w)|\le\frac KN\sum_i\|J_i(z-w)\|\le K\|z-w\|.\]

The same constant survives the averaging because the latent coordinates were scaled by \(N^{-1/2}\).




Let \(\bar f_N(z)=N^{-1}\sum_i f(A_i z)\). Cauchy–Schwarz and the replication identity give \[\frac1N\sum_i\|A_i v\|\le\left(\frac1N\sum_i\|A_i v\|^2\right)^{1/2}=\|v\|.\] Applying \(|f(a)-f(b)|\le K\|a-b\|\) to each pair and summing proves \(|\bar f_N(z)-\bar f_N(w)|\le K\|z-w\|\). The declarations separate the average norm estimate from the value estimate so that the particle scaling can be checked independently.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{averaged\allowbreak{}Potential}: The averaged lifted potential in the physical-time Langevin equation.
\item \texttt{average\_\allowbreak{}joint\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le}: Average pair distances are controlled by the scaled state norm.
\item \texttt{averaged\allowbreak{}Potential\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}: Averaging in the scaled coordinates preserves the pair potential's Lipschitz constant.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Shared-\allowbreak{}noise contraction of the actual PIPGLA chain}{Shared-noise contraction of the actual PIPGLA chain}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingContraction-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Contraction.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Let \(N>0\), let \(g_1\) be differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient, and let \(g_2\) be convex and bounded below. Assume \(0<m\le L_1\), \(\lambda>0\), and \(0\le\gamma\le2/(m+L_1)\). These are deterministic comparisons of two chains with the same innovations.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Jensen handles two independently averaged outputs}{Jensen handles two independently averaged outputs}}


Apply the scalar average-square inequality to the difference of the parameter outputs. Each latent difference has its exact scaled square. Thus

\[\|\operatorname{Pair}_P(z)-\operatorname{Pair}_P(w)\|^2\le N^{-1}\sum_i\|P(J_i z)-P(J_i w)\|^2.\]

If \(P\) is nonexpansive, each summand is bounded by \(\|J_i(z-w)\|^2\). Their sum is \(N\|z-w\|^2\), proving that the actual pairwise map is also nonexpansive.




Put \(A_i z=(\theta,\sqrt N z_i)\) and \(\Delta_i=P(A_i z)-P(A_i w)\). The shared parameter is an average, so Jensen gives \(N\|\overline\Delta_\theta\|^2\le\sum_i\|\Delta_{i,\theta}\|^2\); each latent coordinate is rescaled by \(N^{-1/2}\), so its corresponding squared identity is exact. Summing yields \[\|\operatorname{pairwiseMap}P(z)-\operatorname{pairwiseMap}P(w)\|^2\le\frac1N\sum_i\|\Delta_i\|^2.\] Nonexpansiveness of \(P\) and \(\sum_i\|A_i(z-w)\|^2=N\|z-w\|^2\) prove the claimed state-space bound. The positivity of \(N\) justifies both division and the unscaling.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map\_\allowbreak{}difference\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}le}: Averaging only the shared parameter contracts the squared difference relative to the average of all separate pairwise squared differences.
\item \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map\_\allowbreak{}nonexpansive}: A nonexpansive pair map remains nonexpansive after the actual shared parameter averaging and physical-particle scaling.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A shared innovation cancels before proximity}{A shared innovation cancels before proximity}}


The lifted smooth gradient step has strong convexity \(Nm\), gradient constant \(NL_1\), and step \(\gamma/N\), so its contraction factor is \(1-m\gamma\ge0\). The same Gaussian or deterministic innovation added to both inputs cancels in their difference. Composition with the actual nonexpansive averaged proximal map gives

\[\|T_\lambda(z,\xi)-T_\lambda(w,\xi)\|\le(1-m\gamma)\|z-w\|.\]

Induction on the displayed recursion yields factor \((1-m\gamma)^n\). This proves stability; a separate stationary reference and local-consistency estimate are needed for convergence to a Gibbs target.




In one shared-noise comparison, the additive Gaussian vector cancels before applying the pairwise proximal map. The smooth forward step contracts by \(q=1-m\gamma\); the actual proximal minimizer is nonexpansive, so composing the two leaves the same factor. The step restriction proves \(q\ge0\), which is needed to multiply the induction inequality. Starting with \(e_0=\|z-w\|\), the successor case gives \(e_{k+1}\le q e_k\le q^{k+1}e_0\). This establishes pathwise stability under arbitrary supplied innovations, without stationarity or independence assumptions.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Update\_\allowbreak{}shared\_\allowbreak{}noise\_\allowbreak{}contract}: PIPGLA contracts synchronously driven initial states at the smooth forward-step rate. No stochastic or equilibrium hypothesis is needed.
\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Chain\_\allowbreak{}shared\_\allowbreak{}noise\_\allowbreak{}contract}: Iterating the actual pathwise inequality controls two PIPGLA runs with the same innovations. It supplies stability, rather than a Gibbs bias bound.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Quadratic Hilbert identities and integrated convex energy}{Quadratic Hilbert identities and integrated convex energy}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingL2Energy-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}L2\allowbreak{}Energy.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


The pointwise proximal estimate is lifted to actual \(L^2\) random variables. All scalar energy expectations are proved integrable before distributing integrals or comparing them.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Weighted Young inequality preserves a linear squared contraction}{Weighted Young inequality preserves a linear squared contraction}}


For \(t>0\), expansion and \((t\|v\|-\|r\|)^2\ge0\) prove

\[\|v+r\|^2\le(1+t)\|v\|^2+(1+t^{-1})\|r\|^2.\]

If \(0<t\le1\) and \(\|v\|\le(1-t)\|e\|\), use \((1+t)(1-t)^2\le1-t\). The separate exact identity \(\|v+r+b\|^2=\|v+r\|^2+2\langle v+r+b,b\rangle-\|b\|^2\) keeps an integrated nonsmooth force inside a useful cross term.




Expanding \(\|v+r\|^2\) and using \(2\langle v,r\rangle\le t\|v\|^2+t^{-1}\|r\|^2\) gives the weighted inequality. If \(\|v\|\le(1-t)\|e\|\), \(0<t\le1\), then \((1+t)(1-t)^2\le1-t\). This is the scalar inequality proved in \texttt{hf}; it keeps a linear contraction in the squared error instead of doubling the leading term. The final identity rearranges \(\|v+r+b\|^2\) with its cross term paired against the final error, the form needed for convex-energy cancellation.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}weighted}: A weighted quadratic triangle bound with an explicit positive weight.
\item \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}contraction}: The weighted estimate retains a linear contraction in the squared error.
\item \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}rearranged}: Moving an integrated nonsmooth drift into a cross term avoids a Lipschitz constant for that drift in the consistency estimate.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Justify potential and quadratic expectations}{Justify potential and quadratic expectations}}


For a globally \(K\)-Lipschitz scalar \(f\), center at \(f(0)\); its growth is at most \(K\|X\|\). Hence \(f(X)\in L^2\subset L^1\) on a probability space. The actual Hilbert-space \(L^2\) inner product gives \(\|X\|_2^2=\int\|X(\omega)\|^2dP\), without any arbitrary representative-dependent value.




For a value-Lipschitz observable \(f\), \(|f(X)|\le K\|X\|+|f(0)|\); hence an \(L^2\) state on a probability space gives \(f(X)\in L^2\subset L^1\). The first proof implements this by centering \(f\) at zero. The second identity uses the definition of the \(L^2\) inner product and \(\langle X,X\rangle=\|X\|^2\): \(\|X\|_{L^2}^2=\int\|X(\omega)\|^2dP\). These facts ensure that the energy expressions below are ordinary finite Bochner integrals.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{integrable\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}comp\allowbreak{}Lp}: Every globally Lipschitz scalar observable of an \texttt{L2} random variable is integrable.
\item \texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}integral}: Exact second-moment identity for actual \texttt{L2} random variables.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Integrate the actual deterministic energy premise}{Integrate the actual deterministic energy premise}}


Suppose \(\|T(x)-y\|^2\le\|x-y\|^2-2h(f(x)-f(y))+C\), with \(T,f\) Lipschitz. Apply it pointwise to \(S,Y\in L^2\). The composition representative agrees almost everywhere with \(T(S)\), and every term is integrable. Monotonicity and linearity of the integral yield

\[\|T(S)-Y\|_2^2\le\|S-Y\|_2^2-2h(\mathbb E f(S)-\mathbb E f(Y))+C.\]


Apply the deterministic premise at \((S(\omega),Y(\omega))\). The representative facts for \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Drift} and subtraction identify its left side almost everywhere with \(\|T(S)-Y\|^2\). The local facts \texttt{hn}, \texttt{hf\allowbreak{}S}, \texttt{hf\allowbreak{}Y} prove integrability of both quadratic norms and both potential values. Only then does \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}mono\_\allowbreak{}ae} integrate the inequality and distribute its finite terms: \[\|T(S)-Y\|_2^2\le\|S-Y\|_2^2-2h(\mathbb Ef(S)-\mathbb Ef(Y))+C.\] The constant remains \(C\) because \(P\) has mass one.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}pointwise}: Lift a proved deterministic energy inequality to actual \texttt{L2} random variables. This is an integration lemma; the particle instantiation below proves its pointwise premise from the genuine proximal minimization problem.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Integrate convex supports and split endpoint differences}{Integrate convex supports and split endpoint differences}}


Convex differentiability gives \(\langle S-Y,\nabla e(Y)\rangle\le e(S)-e(Y)\) pointwise. Integrate to obtain the same inequality for the \(L^2\) inner product. For a third variable \(Z\), write \(S-Z=(S-Y)+(Y-Z)\). This splits the cross term into the convex energy difference plus \(\langle Y-Z,\nabla e(Y)\rangle_{L^2}\), whose future-noise part can later be canceled.




The pointwise supporting-plane inequality is \(e(S)-e(Y)\ge\langle\nabla e(Y),S-Y\rangle\). Potential integrability follows from value Lipschitzness; inner-product integrability follows from both vectors belonging to \(L^2\), with score Lipschitzness ensuring \(\nabla e(Y)\in L^2\). Integration gives the first declaration. For an endpoint \(Z\), write \(S-Z=(S-Y)+(Y-Z)\) and use linearity of the inner product. This produces the energy difference plus the reference-increment cross term, precisely the two contributions used in \texttt{splitting\_\allowbreak{}cross\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le}.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}convex\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}support}: The actual convex gradient supports every square-integrable comparison variable after integrating the pointwise supporting-plane inequality.
\item \texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}convex\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}cross}: Endpoint comparison split into the convex energy difference and a reference-increment cross term, all in the genuine \texttt{L2} inner product.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Local smooth drift defect and the future cross term}{Local smooth drift defect and the future cross term}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingProcess-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Process.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Let a continuous Hilbert-valued reference satisfy \(Y_t-Y_0=-\int_0^t g(Y_s)ds+B_t\), with \(\|g(Y_t)\|\le G\) and \(\|B_t\|\le\sigma\sqrt t\). Only the smooth-part Lipschitz constant enters the local defect.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Integrate the smooth drift increment}{Integrate the smooth drift increment}}


The integral equation implies \(\|Y_t-Y_0\|\le Gt+\sigma\sqrt t\). If \(a\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz and \(0\le t\le h\), then \(\|a(Y_t)-a(Y_0)\|\le L(Gh+\sigma\sqrt h)\). Integrating over an interval of length \(h\) yields

\[\left\|\int_0^h(a(Y_t)-a(Y_0))dt\right\|\le Lh(Gh+\sigma\sqrt h).\]


For the integral solution \(Y_t-Y_0=-\int_0^t g(Y_s)ds+B_t\), assume \(\|g(Y_s)\|\le G\) and \(\|B_t\|\le\nu\sqrt t\) in the Hilbert space under consideration. Then \(\|Y_t-Y_0\|\le tG+\nu\sqrt t\). If only the smooth part \(a\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz, \[\left\|\int_0^h(a(Y_t)-a(Y_0))dt\right\|\le Lh(Gh+\nu\sqrt h).\] The code first bounds the integrand uniformly for \(0\le t\le h\), using monotonicity of \(t\) and \(\sqrt t\), and then applies the interval-integral norm inequality. The Lipschitz constant of the full drift ensures regularity but does not enter this numerical bound.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{splitting\_\allowbreak{}local\allowbreak{}Defect\_\allowbreak{}bound}: A coarse local smooth-drift defect bound uses only the smooth part's own Lipschitz constant and the total reference drift moment.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Cancel future noise before estimating the remainder}{Cancel future noise before estimating the remainder}}


Subtract the reference equations at \(t,h\) and use adjacent-interval additivity:

\[Y_t-Y_h=\int_t^h g(Y_s)ds-(B_h-B_t).\]

If \(\|b\|\le K\) and \(\langle b,B_h-B_t\rangle=0\), the noise term cancels exactly. Cauchy–Schwarz and the drift bound give \(\langle Y_t-Y_h,b\rangle\le(h-t)GK\). This avoids an order-\(\sqrt{h-t}\) loss.




Subtract the two integral equations and split the integral at \(t\): \[Y_t-Y_h=\int_t^h g(Y_s)ds-(B_h-B_t).\] Pair this identity with \(b\). The explicit orthogonality assumption cancels the future-noise contribution before any norm estimate, leaving \[\langle Y_t-Y_h,b\rangle\le\left\|\int_t^h g(Y_s)ds\right\|\|b\|\le(h-t)GK.\] In the probabilistic application this is an inner product in \(L^2\), so orthogonality means the expected inner product is zero; it is not a samplewise cancellation of Brownian increments.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{reference\_\allowbreak{}future\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}le}: Future-noise orthogonality turns the reference increment cross term into a drift integral of order \texttt{h-\allowbreak{}t}, rather than a Brownian norm of order \texttt{sqrt(h-\allowbreak{}t)}.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Regroup genuinely independent source blocks}{Regroup genuinely independent source blocks}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-IndependenceHelpers-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Independence\allowbreak{}Helpers.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


These measure-theoretic lemmas reorganize supplied joint independence. They do not infer joint independence from pairwise independence.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Two independent variables form a Boolean family}{Two independent variables form a Boolean family}}


Given \(X\perp Y\), define \(Z_{\rm false}=X\), \(Z_{\rm true}=Y\). The finite-family intersection criterion has four possible index subsets: the empty set, either singleton, or both indices. Probability mass one handles the empty case, the singletons are identities, and the two-element case is exactly \(P(X\in A,Y\in B)=P(X\in A)P(Y\in B)\).




An independent family is defined by factorization for every finite set of indices. For a Boolean index there are four possibilities: neither variable, only \(X\), only \(Y\), or both. The singleton identities are tautological; the empty family has probability one because \(\mu\) is a probability; the two-variable case is exactly the assumed independence of \(X\) and \(Y\). The two membership case splits in Lean exhaust these four possibilities explicitly.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{i\allowbreak{}Indep\allowbreak{}Fun\_\allowbreak{}bool\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}indep\allowbreak{}Fun}: Two independent random variables form an independent family indexed by \texttt{Bool}. The probability assumption supplies the empty-family identity.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Regroup a jointly independent triple}{Regroup a jointly independent triple}}


Assume \(X\perp(Y,Z)\) and \(Y\perp Z\), with measurable variables. The joint pushforward is \(\mu_X\otimes(\mu_Y\otimes\mu_Z)\). Use the measurable product-associativity equivalence to rewrite it as \((\mu_X\otimes\mu_Y)\otimes\mu_Z\). Projection also proves \(\mu_{(X,Y)}=\mu_X\otimes\mu_Y\), so \((X,Y)\perp Z\). The joint independence premise is essential.




The hypotheses say that \(X\) is independent of the whole pair \((Y,Z)\), and that \(Y\) and \(Z\) are independent. Therefore the triple law is \(\mu_X\otimes(\mu_Y\otimes\mu_Z)\). Product associativity rewrites it as \((\mu_X\otimes\mu_Y)\otimes\mu_Z\), proving independence of \((X,Y)\) and \(Z\). The proof uses equality of pushforward measures under the measurable associator, so this is joint independence; pairwise independence of three unrelated variables would not suffice.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{indep\allowbreak{}Fun\_\allowbreak{}prod\allowbreak{}Mk\_\allowbreak{}left\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}indep\allowbreak{}Fun}: Regroup a jointly independent triple from \texttt{X | (Y,Z)} into \texttt{(X,Y) | Z}. Independence of \texttt{Y} and \texttt{Z} is essential; pairwise independence alone is insufficient.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Solutions depend measurably on their initial state and past noise}{Solutions depend measurably on their initial state and past noise}}
\label{proof-module-UnadjustedLangevin-WeakForwardAdapted-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Unadjusted\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Weak\allowbreak{}Forward\allowbreak{}Adapted.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


The integral solution remains in any complete invariant subspace containing the initial state and noise. Applying this principle to the subspace of random variables measurable with respect to a smaller sigma algebra proves the needed adaptedness statement.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{An invariant-\allowbreak{}subspace principle}{An invariant-subspace principle}}


Solve the same integral equation inside the complete submodule \(S\), using drift invariance, \(x\in S\), and \(B(t)\in S\). Include this solution back into the ambient space; continuous linearity of the inclusion commutes with the Bochner integral. Ambient uniqueness identifies it with \(Y\), so every \(Y(t)\) lies in \(S\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{lp\allowbreak{}Drift aestrongly\allowbreak{}Measurable}{lpDrift aestronglyMeasurable}}


A Lipschitz map is continuous, so composing it with an almost-everywhere strongly measurable \(X\) preserves that measurability for the specified sub-sigma-algebra. The representative of the lifted drift agrees almost everywhere with this composition. Transport across that equality proves the result independently of the representative selected for the \(L^2\) class.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{lp langevin integral aestrongly\allowbreak{}Measurable}{lp langevin integral aestronglyMeasurable}}


The \(L^2\) variables measurable for a sub-sigma-algebra form a complete closed submodule. The preceding composition theorem makes that submodule invariant under the lifted drift. Apply submodule preservation using measurability of the initialization and every noise value up to \(T\); the integral solution then belongs to the same submodule at each time.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{lp\allowbreak{}Langevin\allowbreak{}Past}{lpLangevinPast}}


Define the past sigma-algebra as the join of the sigma-algebra generated by the initial variable and those generated by \(B(t)\), \(0\le t\le T\). Lean uses \texttt{Measurable\allowbreak{}Space.\allowbreak{}comap} and suprema to construct this information explicitly from the chosen measurable representatives.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{lp\allowbreak{}Langevin\allowbreak{}Past le}{lpLangevinPast le}}


Every generator of the past is measurable in the ambient sigma-algebra. The universal property of a supremum of sigma-algebras therefore gives \(\mathcal F_T\le\mathcal F\). This inclusion is required to use the closed \(L^2\) subspace of variables measurable from the past.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{lp\allowbreak{}Langevin\allowbreak{}Past initial}{lpLangevinPast initial}}


The sigma-algebra generated by the initial variable is one summand of the defining join for \(\mathcal F_T\). Its inclusion makes the initial variable measurable for \(\mathcal F_T\), and the measurable representative of an \(L^2\) variable supplies the almost-everywhere strong-measurability formulation used by the submodule theorem.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{lp\allowbreak{}Langevin\allowbreak{}Past noise}{lpLangevinPast noise}}


For \(t\in[0,T]\), the sigma-algebra generated by \(B(t)\) is included in the indexed join defining the past. Measurability of the representative with respect to its generated sigma-algebra then extends along this inclusion, giving \(B(t)\)'s measurability from \(\mathcal F_T\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{lp langevin integral natural\allowbreak{}Past}{lp langevin integral naturalPast}}


Apply the sub-sigma-algebra solution theorem to the explicitly generated \(\mathcal F_T\). The three preceding declarations discharge its ambient inclusion, initialization, and noise-measurability premises. Thus every solution value up to \(T\) is measurable from initialization and past noise, with no adaptedness assumption added to the solution.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Derive Brownian future orthogonality from the actual past}{Derive Brownian future orthogonality from the actual past}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingOrthogonality-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Orthogonality.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Let the scalar Brownian coordinates have independent full paths, measurable evaluations, and initialization independent of the whole Brownian family. The actual Langevin integral solution is measurable from initialization and the stopped noise past.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Assemble independent vector past and shifted future}{Assemble independent vector past and shifted future}}


Each scalar Brownian path has independent shifted future \(B_i(T+s)-B_i(T)\) and stopped past \(B_i(\min(t,T))\). Encode these as a Boolean pair for each coordinate. Full-path independence makes the coordinate rows independent; flatten the rows and Boolean columns, then take the disjoint sets of past and future indices. Regrouping proves independence of the entire vector path families.




The stopped past records each coordinate path up to time \(t\), while the future block records increments after \(t\). Brownian independent increments give this separation within each scalar coordinate. Independence of the whole coordinate paths permits regrouping the coordinatewise pairs into one vector past and one vector future. This is a statement about the generated product sigma-algebras, so it remains valid when the future vector is paired with any measurable function of the entire past.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{brownian\_\allowbreak{}future\_\allowbreak{}past\_\allowbreak{}independent}: The complete shifted future and the stopped past of independent scalar Brownian coordinates are independent vector-valued path families.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Relate concrete noise to its L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} representative}{Relate concrete noise to its L² representative}}


Define the pointwise vector \(\mathcal B_t=\sqrt2(B(t_+)-B(0))\), using the nonnegative-time conversion \(t_+=\max(t,0)\). The \(L^2\) Brownian-noise construction agrees almost everywhere with this formula. Each coordinate evaluation is measurable, so finite vector assembly, subtraction and scaling prove measurability of the raw representative.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{raw\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Noise}: The pointwise vector presentation of the Langevin noise from time zero.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Add the independent initialization to the stopped past}{Add the independent initialization to the stopped past}}


The stopped noise path and a future increment are independent. Initialization is independent of their joint pair, so the regrouping lemma proves that initialization together with the stopped past is independent of the future. An auxiliary result then compares sigma-algebras: every variable almost everywhere strongly measurable for the natural initialization/\allowbreak{}noise past inherits this independence. Almost everywhere replacement transfers the result to actual \(L^2\) representatives.




The initialized natural past contains both the initial state and the stopped Brownian path. Independence of initialization from the entire Brownian source, together with past/\allowbreak{}future Brownian independence, permits the triple regrouping \((\text{initial},\text{past})\mid\text{future}\). An almost-strongly-measurable past variable can be replaced by a measurable representative without changing its law or independence. This is the role of the representative arguments: independence is transferred through almost-everywhere equality, rather than assumed for the later integral solution.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{brownian\_\allowbreak{}future\_\allowbreak{}independent\_\allowbreak{}initial\_\allowbreak{}past}: A future Brownian increment is independent of the initialization and all earlier actual \texttt{L2} Brownian-noise representatives.
\item \texttt{natural\allowbreak{}Past\_\allowbreak{}independent\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}aestrongly\allowbreak{}Measurable}: Every random variable measurable from the natural initialization/\allowbreak{}noise past inherits its independence from a supplied future source variable.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Centering and independence imply Hilbert orthogonality}{Centering and independence imply Hilbert orthogonality}}


Every scalar Brownian coordinate has mean zero. Commute the finite coordinate projections through the Bochner integral to get \(\mathbb E B_t=0\), then subtract and scale for \(\mathbb E\mathcal B_t=0\). For independent \(L^2\) variables \(X,W\), bilinear integration gives \(\langle X,W\rangle_{L^2}=\langle\mathbb E X,\mathbb E W\rangle\). If \(W\) is centered this is zero.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{brownian\allowbreak{}Coordinates\_\allowbreak{}integral\_\allowbreak{}zero}: The finite-dimensional Brownian vector has zero mean.
\item \texttt{brownian\allowbreak{}Noise\_\allowbreak{}integral\_\allowbreak{}zero}: The actual \texttt{L2} additive Langevin noise is centered at every real time.
\item \texttt{independent\_\allowbreak{}centered\_\allowbreak{}lp\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}zero}: Binary independence and zero mean imply genuine \texttt{L2} orthogonality.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Apply adaptedness of the constructed integral solution}{Apply adaptedness of the constructed integral solution}}


The invariant-subspace construction for the actual integral equation proves that \(Y_t\) is measurable for the natural past. A Lipschitz observable \(b(Y_t)\) remains past measurable and in \(L^2\). The preceding independence and zero-mean results therefore prove

\[\langle b(Y_t),\mathcal B_h-\mathcal B_t\rangle_{L^2}=0,\qquad0\le t\le h.\]

This essential cancellation is a conclusion of the Brownian and integral-equation construction.




The constructed solution is adapted to the initialized natural past, so the Lipschitz observable \(b(Y_t)\) is measurable there. The future Brownian increment is independent of this past and has zero mean. Both variables belong to \(L^2\), hence their inner product is integrable, and independence factors its expectation as \[\mathbb E\langle b(Y_t),B_h-B_t\rangle=\left\langle\mathbb E b(Y_t),\mathbb E(B_h-B_t)\right\rangle=0.\] The \(L^2\) inner product is precisely this integral. Thus the final orthogonality input of the splitting proof follows from adaptedness, centering and the constructed Brownian law.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}langevin\_\allowbreak{}future\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}zero}: A Lipschitz function of the actual Langevin solution is orthogonal to every later Brownian increment. Adaptedness is derived from the integral equation by the existing invariant-subspace construction.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Cancel the original nonsmooth energy in expectation}{Cancel the original nonsmooth energy in expectation}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCrossEnergy-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Cross\allowbreak{}Energy.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Let \(e\) be convex and differentiable, let \(e\le f\le e+\delta\), and let \(Y_t\) be a stationary reference for the smoothed energy with the actual integral equation and future-noise orthogonality. Lipschitz value bounds ensure all expectations exist.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Integrate a one-\allowbreak{}sided uniform value gap}{Integrate a one-sided uniform value gap}}


If \(f(x)\le e(x)+\delta\) pointwise and both functions are Lipschitz, their compositions with any \(L^2\) variable \(X\) are integrable. Integral monotonicity and total mass one give \(\mathbb E f(X)-\mathbb E e(X)\le\delta\). This is the precise one-sided expectation lemma used in the cross-energy argument.




If \(f\le e+\delta\), the two Lipschitz observables of an \(L^2\) state are integrable. Thus \(\mathbb Ef(X)\le\mathbb Ee(X)+\delta P(\Omega)=\mathbb Ee(X)+\delta\). The proof checks this integrability before splitting the integral. This comparison will be used once at the algorithm's intermediate state with \(e\le f\), and once at the reference endpoint with \(f\le e+\delta\).




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{expectation\_\allowbreak{}gap\_\allowbreak{}le}: A finite uniform value gap controls its actual expectation.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Combine convex support, stationarity and the future estimate}{Combine convex support, stationarity and the future estimate}}


The integrated convex support bounds \(\langle S-Y_h,\nabla e(Y_t)\rangle\) by \(\mathbb E e(S)-\mathbb E e(Y_t)+\langle Y_t-Y_h,\nabla e(Y_t)\rangle\). Stationarity replaces \(\mathbb E e(Y_t)\) by \(\mathbb E e(Y_h)\). Orthogonality and bounded drift/\allowbreak{}score give the remaining term at most \(hGK\). Commute the fixed inner-product functional through the time integral. Finally the gap \(e\le f\le e+\delta\) changes the two expected energies by at most \(\delta\), proving

\[2\left\langle S-Y_h,\int_0^h\nabla e(Y_t)dt\right\rangle-2h(\mathbb E f(S)-\mathbb E f(Y_h))\le2h\delta+2h^2GK.\]
