For \(b_t=\nabla e(Y_t)\) in \(L^2\), convex support and the future-increment estimate imply \[\langle S-Y_h,b_t\rangle\le\mathbb Ee(S)-\mathbb Ee(Y_t)+(h-t)GK.\] Stationarity replaces \(\mathbb Ee(Y_t)\) by \(\mathbb Ee(Y_h)\). Integrate on \([0,h]\); continuity of \(b_t\) justifies moving the bounded linear inner-product functional through the Bochner integral. This yields an upper bound \(h[\mathbb Ee(S)-\mathbb Ee(Y_h)]+h^2GK\).



The two value comparisons give \([\mathbb Ee(S)-\mathbb Ee(Y_h)]-[\mathbb Ef(S)-\mathbb Ef(Y_h)]\le\delta\). Multiply by \(2h\) and combine to obtain the displayed \(2h\delta+2h^2GK\) remainder. In Lean, \texttt{hpoint} is the pointwise-in-time estimate, \texttt{hi} its integral, and \texttt{hsum} the finite value-gap cancellation. The conclusion never substitutes \(\operatorname{Lip}(\nabla e)\) into this remainder.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{splitting\_\allowbreak{}cross\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le}: The integrated reference-gradient cross term cancels the original nonsmooth energy up to a finite value gap and a drift error of order \texttt{h\ensuremath{{}^{2}}}. The orthogonality premise is instantiated by the actual Brownian construction in \texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}langevin\_\allowbreak{}future\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}zero}, not by a convergence assumption.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Derive the synchronous one-\allowbreak{}step splitting bound}{Derive the synchronous one-step splitting bound}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingOneStep-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}One\allowbreak{}Step.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


In a Hilbert \(L^2\) space, let \(f_1\) be \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient, and let the actual Lipschitz map \(T\) satisfy the nonsmooth energy inequality for \(f\). The reference solves the actual stationary smoothed integral equation for \(f_1+e\), where \(e\le f\le e+\delta\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Keep every local forcing term explicit}{Keep every local forcing term explicit}}


For \(m,h>0\), drift moment \(G\), noise moment factor \(\sigma\), bounded nonsmooth score \(K\) and value gap \(\delta\), define

\[\mathcal F=(1+(mh)^{-1})[Lh(Gh+\sigma\sqrt h)]^2+2h\delta+2h^2GK+h^2K^2.\]

The four terms are the weighted smooth local defect, the envelope value gap, the drift cross term, and the genuine proximal energy remainder.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{splitting\allowbreak{}Coupling\allowbreak{}Forcing}: Explicit local forcing in the synchronous splitting proof.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{An exact decomposition makes the energy cancel}{An exact decomposition makes the energy cancel}}


Let \(S=X-h\nabla f_1(X)+B_h\), \(v=(X-h\nabla f_1(X))-(Y_0-h\nabla f_1(Y_0))\), \(R=\int_0^h[\nabla f_1(Y_t)-\nabla f_1(Y_0)]dt\), and \(H=\int_0^h\nabla e(Y_t)dt\). The integral equation gives the exact identity \(S-Y_h=v+R+H\). Expand its square using the rearranged identity; integrate the actual proximal energy inequality for \(T(S)\). The cross-energy lemma cancels the resulting expected \(f\)-difference. Sharp smooth-step contraction and weighted Young inequality bound \(\|v+R\|^2\), while the local-defect lemma bounds \(R\). Discarding \(-\|H\|^2\le0\) yields

\[\|T(S)-Y_h\|_2^2\le(1-mh)\|X-Y_0\|_2^2+\mathcal F.\]

Here \(m\le L\) and \(h\le2/(m+L)\) imply \(mh\le1\). Every reference premise is later supplied by the canonical construction.




Set \(a=\nabla f_1\), \(b=\nabla e\), \(S=X-ha(X)+B_h\), \(v=[X-ha(X)]-[Y_0-ha(Y_0)]\), \(R=\int_0^h[a(Y_t)-a(Y_0)]dt\), and \(H=\int_0^h b(Y_t)dt\). Splitting the actual reference integral equation gives the exact identity \(S-Y_h=v+R+H\). The norm rearrangement is \[\|S-Y_h\|^2=\|v+R\|^2+2\langle S-Y_h,H\rangle-\|H\|^2.\]



Apply the genuine proximal energy estimate to \(T(S)\), then control \(\|v+R\|^2\) using the weighted inequality with \(t=mh\). Strong smooth convexity gives \(\|v\|\le(1-mh)\|X-Y_0\|\), and the step restriction proves \(mh\le1\). The local smooth drift estimate bounds \(\|R\|\), while the preceding cross-energy theorem cancels the original nonsmooth potential difference up to \(2h\delta+2h^2GK\). Discarding \(-\|H\|^2\le0\) leaves exactly the four displayed terms in \texttt{splitting\allowbreak{}Coupling\allowbreak{}Forcing}. The facts \texttt{hdecomp}, \texttt{hweighted}, \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Rweighted} and \texttt{hcross} correspond to these four stages.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{splitting\_\allowbreak{}one\_\allowbreak{}step\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}integral\_\allowbreak{}solution}: One splitting step compared with an actual smooth stationary reference. The pointwise energy premise is proved for the actual averaged joint proximal map; the reference hypotheses are discharged by its canonical Brownian construction. No stochastic error recurrence is assumed.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Take the infimum of squared finite coupling estimates}{Take the infimum of squared finite coupling estimates}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingTransport-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Transport.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Assume \(W_2(\mu,\nu)<\infty\), \(q,c\ge0\), and \(W_2(\mu',\nu')^2\le q\operatorname{cost}(p)^2+c\) for every finite-cost coupling \(p\) of \(\mu,\nu\). No optimal coupling is needed.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Use the greatest lower bound characterization}{Use the greatest lower bound characterization}}


If \(q=0\), one finite coupling already gives the result. If the target square is at most \(c\), the conclusion is immediate. Otherwise set \(r=\sqrt{(W_2(\mu',\nu')^2-c)/q}\). Each coupling estimate implies \(r\le\operatorname{cost}(p)\); infinite-cost couplings also satisfy this extended inequality. Hence \(r\le\inf_p\operatorname{cost}(p)=W_2(\mu,\nu)\). Square this nonnegative inequality and multiply by \(q\) to obtain

\[W_2(\mu',\nu')^2\le qW_2(\mu,\nu)^2+c.\]

Finiteness justifies every extended-to-real conversion.




Write \(A=W_2(\mu',\nu')^2\), and suppose every finite-cost input coupling satisfies \(A\le qC^2+c\). If \(q=0\), one finite-cost coupling already proves \(A\le c\). If \(A\le c\), the result is immediate from \(q\ge0\). Otherwise set \(r=\sqrt{(A-c)/q}\); then every finite coupling has cost at least \(r\), and infinite-cost couplings also satisfy this lower bound in the extended order.



The operation \texttt{le\_\allowbreak{}i\allowbreak{}Inf} now proves \(r\le W_2(\mu,\nu)\) directly from the definition as an infimum. The finite-distance hypothesis justifies passing to real values. Squaring and multiplying by \(q\) gives \(A\le qW_2(\mu,\nu)^2+c\). This is why the proof does not need existence of an optimal transport plan.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{wasserstein2\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}bound\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}finite\_\allowbreak{}couplings}: A uniform squared estimate for actual finite-cost couplings passes to the square of the genuine Wasserstein distance.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Construct a stationary Brownian coupling for one true transition}{Construct a stationary Brownian coupling for one true transition}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCanonical-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Canonical.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


The generic splitting map is \(\mathcal T_h(x,\xi)=T(x-h\nabla f_1(x)+\sqrt{2h}\xi)\). The reference target is the actual Gibbs law of \(F=f_1+e\). Assumptions concern potentials, true energy, bounded gradients and a finite input coupling.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Measurable update and exact gradient additivity}{Measurable update and exact gradient additivity}}


The map is the composition of the measurable Gaussian forward update with the Lipschitz map \(T\). Differentiability proves \(\nabla(f_1+e)=\nabla f_1+\nabla e\). Almost everywhere representative identities then prove additivity of the corresponding \(L^2\) drift maps, as an equality of actual \(L^2\) elements.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{splitting\allowbreak{}Update}: The actual forward, noise, backward splitting transition.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Drift\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}add}: The lifted drift of an actual sum is the sum of its lifted gradients.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Extend any finite-\allowbreak{}cost coupling by canonical Brownian noise}{Extend any finite-cost coupling by canonical Brownian noise}}


Start with any coupling \(c\) between \(\mu\) and the Gibbs target, with finite quadratic cost. Extend it by the canonical independent Brownian source. The initial reference has the exact Gibbs law and finite moments; the constructed Langevin integral solution stays stationary. Its natural-past measurability and Brownian independence prove the future orthogonality. Uniform moments give

\[G=\sqrt{2L^2d/m+2K^2}+K,\qquad\sigma=\sqrt{2d}.\]

Use the Brownian increment over \([0,h]\) as the Gaussian update noise; its independence gives the exact Markov marginal. Apply the derived analytic one-step theorem. The explicit output pair is a finite coupling and proves

\[W_2(\mu\mathcal T_h,\pi)^2\le(1-mh)\operatorname{cost}(c)^2+\mathcal F(m,h,L,G,\sigma,K,\delta).\]

The construction discharges stationarity, Gaussian laws, finite moments and orthogonality; they are not extra convergence hypotheses.




Start from an arbitrary finite-cost coupling \(c\) between the input law and \(\pi\propto e^{-(f_1+e)}\). Extend it by an independent canonical Brownian source, producing square-integrable initial states \(X,Z\) with the required marginals. The actual Lipschitz integral equation constructs \(Y_t\) from \(Z\), and the Gibbs stationarity theorem proves \(\operatorname{Law}(Y_t)=\pi\) throughout the interval.



The bound on the total reference force is \[G=\sqrt{2L_1^2d/m+2K^2}+K,\] combining the proved smooth-score Gibbs moment with the uniformly bounded envelope score. The Brownian moment gives \(\|B_t\|_2\le\sqrt{2d}\sqrt t\). Adaptedness and future-increment independence prove \texttt{horth}; stationarity proves equality of the envelope expectations in \texttt{hstationary}. These facts discharge the analytic one-step theorem's stochastic inputs.



Finally, the normalized Brownian increment \(V\) has the standard Gaussian law and is independent of \(X\). The representative identity \texttt{h\allowbreak{}X'coe} identifies the updated random variable with \(T(X-h\nabla f_1(X)+\sqrt{2h}V)\); \texttt{h\allowbreak{}X'law} proves its law is the actual independent-Gaussian Markov step. The pair \((X',Y_h)\) therefore provides a finite transport coupling. This proves both output finiteness and the squared estimate for the original arbitrary \(c\).




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{splitting\_\allowbreak{}markov\_\allowbreak{}step\_\allowbreak{}coupling\_\allowbreak{}bound}: A genuine canonical coupling estimate for one actual independent Gaussian splitting step. All stochastic inputs of the analytic one-step theorem are constructed here from the potential assumptions.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Actual splitting laws, L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} realizations and iid law transfer}{Actual splitting laws, L² realizations and iid law transfer}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingLaw-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Law.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


The pointwise splitting update applies the averaged true proximal map after a noisy smooth step. Convexity and a lower bound for \(g_2\), positive \(\lambda\), \(N>0\) and a Lipschitz smooth gradient make this update measurable and preserve finite quadratic moments.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Prove measurability of the exact pointwise update}{Prove measurability of the exact pointwise update}}


Nonexpansiveness proves \(\operatorname{Lip}(\operatorname{Pair}_{P_\lambda})\le1\). The forward map \((z,\xi)\mapsto z-(\gamma/N)\nabla F_{g_1}(z)+\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\xi\) is measurable by continuity of its terms; compose it with proximity. Induction proves equality of the custom pointwise chain with the generic noise-driven chain.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Update\_\allowbreak{}measurable}: The exact splitting update is jointly measurable in the state and noise.
\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Chain\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}noise\allowbreak{}Driven\allowbreak{}Chain}: The pointwise recursion is the existing generic noise-driven chain.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Realize the actual chain inside L\ensuremath{{}^{2}}}{Realize the actual chain inside L²}}


Define the algorithm law as the pushforward by the explicit chain. Define an \(L^2\) update by applying the Lipschitz pairwise map to the \(L^2\) noisy Euler update. Their representatives agree almost everywhere by the composition and addition identities in \(L^2\). Inductively every iterate lies in \(L^2\) and agrees almost everywhere with its pointwise recursion. This constructs moment control without Gaussianity or equilibrium assumptions.




The square-integrable update first applies the Euler drift/\allowbreak{}noise map and then the genuine pairwise proximal map. Both are Lipschitz, so the result is again an \(L^2\) random variable. The update representative theorem intersects the full-measure sets for these two operations and obtains the pointwise PIPGLA formula. An induction repeats this at each fixed finite time, replacing the previous representative by the pointwise iterate on their common full-measure event. The chain's pushforward law is therefore exactly \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Laws}, rather than merely a convenient auxiliary recursion.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Laws}: The law of the actual PIPGLA state after a supplied innovation sequence.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Pipgla\allowbreak{}Update}: The actual noisy forward step followed by the genuine pairwise proximal map, represented in L\ensuremath{{}^{2}}.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Pipgla\allowbreak{}Chain}: Every iterate is an actual L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} random variable.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Pushforward equality proves mass one}{Pushforward equality proves mass one}}


Almost everywhere equal representatives have equal pushforward measures, so \(\rho_n=Q\circ X_n^{-1}\) for the constructed \(L^2\) iterate. Its almost everywhere measurability makes this a genuine probability law: the preimage of the whole state space has \(Q\)-mass one.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Transfer any iid realization to the canonical product law}{Transfer any iid realization to the canonical product law}}


Assume the initial variable is independent of the whole innovation path and the innovations form an independent family with common law \(\eta\). The joint input law is \(\mathcal L(X_0)\otimes\eta^{\otimes\mathbb N}\). Applying the measurable noise-driven recursion gives exactly the canonical chain law. The proof uses the actual independence and common-law hypotheses, without assuming a stochastic error estimate.




For a measurable update, the time-\(n\) state is a measurable function of initialization and the innovation path. Independence of initialization from that path makes their joint law a product; iid innovations make the path law \(\eta^{\otimes\mathbb N}\). Pushing this product through the finite recursion proves the canonical-law equality. The assumptions \texttt{hinit}, \texttt{hnoise}, \texttt{hind}, \texttt{hiid} and \texttt{hlaw} respectively supply these measurability, independence and distribution facts; none is a convergence-rate premise.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}canonical}: Any independent iid realization has the canonical splitting law. This proves law transfer across coupling spaces without a convergence assumption.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Canonical Gaussian law of the actual splitting algorithm}{Canonical Gaussian law of the actual splitting algorithm}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingGaussianLaw-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Gaussian\allowbreak{}Law.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


The scalar-coordinate Gaussian hypotheses determine the complete vector innovation law and its infinite iid path. Hence every realization with a fixed initial law gives the same actual PIPGLA output law.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Assemble scalar coordinates into iid standard Gaussian vectors}{Assemble scalar coordinates into iid standard Gaussian vectors}}


The coordinate laws and their joint independence imply \(\xi_k\sim\mathcal N(0,I)\) as a full state vector, and independence of these vectors across \(k\). Together with initialization/\allowbreak{}path independence, the generic iid transfer theorem gives the chain law driven by \(\mathcal N(0,I)^{\otimes\mathbb N}\).




The hypothesis \texttt{Gaussian\allowbreak{}Innovations} specifies laws and independence of all scalar coordinates over all times, as well as independence of initialization from the whole path. \texttt{gaussian\allowbreak{}Innovations\_\allowbreak{}vector\allowbreak{}Law} groups a finite set of independent standard scalar Gaussians into a standard state-space Gaussian; \texttt{gaussian\allowbreak{}Innovations\_\allowbreak{}vector\allowbreak{}Independent} groups the time slices into an independent vector family. The measurable recursion can then be pushed forward from the product of the initial law and this iid path law. Equality follows for the actual PIPGLA iterate at every finite time; it requires no invariant target.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}canonical\allowbreak{}Gaussian}: The full iid vector Gaussian algorithm law follows from the original scalar-coordinate Gaussian hypotheses.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Use either canonical noise-\allowbreak{}path representation}{Use either canonical noise-path representation}}


The countable product of scalar Gaussian coordinates, grouped by iteration, has the same noise-path pushforward law as the vector product. Apply the measurable chain to this identity. Two probability-space realizations with the same initialization law therefore both equal the same canonical Gaussian chain law at time \(n\), proving equality of their actual output laws.




There are two descriptions of the same noise source: a countable product of scalar coordinates, packed into vectors, or a countable product of standard vector Gaussians. The first law identity uses \texttt{noise\allowbreak{}Path\_\allowbreak{}law} directly; the second uses vector iid laws. To compare two probability spaces, rewrite both output laws in the vector representation and substitute the equal initial laws. The ambient probability spaces and their concrete random-variable representatives therefore disappear from the conclusion.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}canonical\allowbreak{}Noise\allowbreak{}Path}: An equivalent canonical law uses the repository's single countable product of scalar Gaussian coordinates, grouped into vector innovations.
\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}initial\allowbreak{}Law\_\allowbreak{}eq}: Gaussian realizations on different probability spaces give identical actual PIPGLA output laws when the initialization laws agree.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Uniform Moreau gaps in scaled particle coordinates}{Uniform Moreau gaps in scaled particle coordinates}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ParticleSmoothing-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Particle\allowbreak{}Smoothing.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Write \(F_g(z)=\sum_i g(J_i z)\), with \(N>0\). If \(g\) is convex, bounded below and \(K\)-Lipschitz, its lifted envelope term has a bounded gradient independent of the positive Moreau scale.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Lift value Lipschitzness and a bounded envelope score}{Lift value Lipschitzness and a bounded envelope score}}


The averaged potential is \(K\)-Lipschitz, so multiplying it by \(N\) proves \(\operatorname{Lip}(F_g)\le NK\). The envelope has the same value Lipschitz constant \(K\), hence \(F_{g^\eta}\) has constant \(NK\) too. Its actual gradient norm is therefore at most \(NK\), uniformly over \(\eta>0\).




Multiplying the preceding averaged-potential inequality by \(N\) proves that the lifted sum has value Lipschitz constant \(NK\). The true Moreau envelope has the same value Lipschitz constant \(K\), so its lifted score satisfies \(\|\nabla V_{N,\eta}^{(2)}\|\le NK\). This follows from the norm of the derivative of a globally Lipschitz function, not from its score Lipschitz constant \(N/\eta\). Consequently this magnitude bound remains uniform as \(\eta\downarrow0\).




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{potential\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}: Lifting a globally Lipschitz pair potential to the scaled state multiplies its Lipschitz constant by the number of particles.
\item \texttt{potential\_\allowbreak{}moreau\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}: The lifted true Moreau approximation preserves the particle Lipschitz constant, uniformly over positive smoothing scales.
\item \texttt{potential\_\allowbreak{}moreau\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le}: The actual gradient of the lifted Moreau term is bounded by \texttt{N*K}, independently of the smoothing scale.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Sum the finite gap and preserve the split decomposition}{Sum the finite gap and preserve the split decomposition}}


Apply \(0\le g-g^\eta\le\eta K^2/2\) at every joint pair and add:

\[0\le F_g(z)-F_{g^\eta}(z)\le N\eta K^2/2.\]

Finite sum distributivity also proves \(F_{g_1+g_2^\eta}=F_{g_1}+F_{g_2^\eta}\) as an equality of functions. These exact identities supply the deterministic inputs to the stochastic splitting estimate.




At every pair, \(0\le g_2-g_2^\eta\le\eta K^2/2\). The finite sum has exactly \(N\) terms, giving \[0\le V_N^{(2)}-V_{N,\eta}^{(2)}\le N\eta K^2/2.\] The decomposition lemma is equality of functions: expand \(V_N(g_1+g_2^\eta)\) and distribute the finite sum to obtain \(V_N(g_1)+V_N(g_2^\eta)\). This identifies the two gradient pieces used by the reference process with the actual smoothed potential.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{potential\_\allowbreak{}moreau\_\allowbreak{}gap}: The finite one-sided Moreau gap for the actual sum over particles.
\item \texttt{potential\_\allowbreak{}smoothed\_\allowbreak{}decomposition}: Lifting commutes with the smooth-plus-Moreau decomposition.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{A finite coefficient for the splitting error floor}{A finite coefficient for the splitting error floor}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingRate-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Rate.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


The derived forcing uses no inverse envelope gradient constant. This scalar estimate turns it into an \(O(h^2)\) one-step squared error when the value gap is \(\delta\le hK^2/2\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Define and sign the explicit coefficient}{Define and sign the explicit coefficient}}


For \(m>0\), \(G,K\ge0\), define

\[C(m,L,G,\sigma,K)=\frac{4L^2}{m^2}\left(\frac{G^2}{m}+\sigma^2\right)+\frac2m(GK+K^2).\]

Squares and positive denominators prove \(C\ge0\). This constant may be conservative but remains finite under its stated scalar assumptions.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{splitting\allowbreak{}RMSConstant}: A finite coefficient for the squared splitting error floor.
\item \texttt{splitting\allowbreak{}RMSConstant\_\allowbreak{}nonneg}: The coefficient is nonnegative under the actual moment-bound hypotheses.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Bound the exact local forcing}{Bound the exact local forcing}}


For \(h\le1/m\), use \((Gh+\sigma\sqrt h)^2\le2G^2h^2+2\sigma^2h\le2h(G^2/m+\sigma^2)\) and \(h+1/m\le2/m\). The weighted defect is then at most \(4L^2h^2(G^2/m+\sigma^2)/m\). The gap contributes \(2h\delta\le h^2K^2\). Adding the remaining \(2h^2GK+h^2K^2\) proves \(\mathcal F\le mh^2C\). Thus iteration can produce the squared floor \(hC\).




The local defect contains \((Gh+\nu\sqrt h)^2\). First use \((u+v)^2\le2u^2+2v^2\) and \(h\le1/m\) to obtain \[(Gh+\nu\sqrt h)^2\le2h(G^2/m+\nu^2).\] Next, \((1+(mh)^{-1})h^2\cdot2h=2h^2(h+1/m)\le4h^2/m\). The finite value gap contributes \(2h\delta\le h^2K^2\); together with the pre-existing \(h^2K^2\) and \(2h^2GK\), these terms give exactly \(mh^2\,\texttt{splittingRMSConstant}\). Every denominator is protected by \(m,h>0\), and the coefficient has no inverse smoothing parameter.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{splitting\allowbreak{}Coupling\allowbreak{}Forcing\_\allowbreak{}le}: With a gap of at most \texttt{h*K\ensuremath{{}^{2}}/\allowbreak{}2}, the derived one-step forcing is bounded by \texttt{m*h\ensuremath{{}^{2}}*C}, where \texttt{C} is independent of the smoothing parameter.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Actual equal-\allowbreak{}step PIPGLA transport convergence}{Actual equal-step PIPGLA transport convergence}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingEndpoint-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Endpoint.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


For \(f_1\) strongly convex and smooth, a bounded convex envelope term \(e\), and a genuine energy map \(T\), this module first proves convergence of actual Gaussian Markov laws. It then instantiates the result with the true averaged particle proximity and equal proximal/\allowbreak{}forward steps.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Derive the one-\allowbreak{}step transport recurrence}{Derive the one-step transport recurrence}}


The admissibility condition and \(m\le L\) give \(mh\le1\). Canonical stationary coupling proves a finite output cost for every finite input coupling. Taking their infimum yields both finiteness and

\[W_2(\rho_{k+1},\pi)^2\le(1-mh)W_2(\rho_k,\pi)^2+\mathcal F.\]

The nonnegative forcing follows from \(e\le f\le e+\delta\), which itself implies \(\delta\ge0\).




The smooth admissibility condition and \(m\le L_1\) imply \(0<mh\le1\), so \(q=1-mh\ge0\). The canonical coupling theorem supplies the same forcing for every finite-cost initial coupling. Taking the coupling infimum yields the actual one-step inequality \[W_2(\mu P,\pi)^2\le qW_2(\mu,\pi)^2+\mathcal F.\] Existence of one finite-cost initial coupling proves finiteness of the output distance at the same time; no optimal coupling is chosen.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{splitting\_\allowbreak{}markov\_\allowbreak{}step\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}bound}: The squared transport recurrence for one actual independent Gaussian transition, with canonical stationarity and the coupling infimum discharged.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Iterate and retain finite moments at every time}{Iterate and retain finite moments at every time}}


Inductively each measurable Markov law is a probability measure and remains at finite distance from the Gibbs reference. If \(\delta\le hK^2/2\), the derived forcing is at most \(mh^2C\). Apply the geometric recurrence with \(q=1-mh\in[0,1)\):

\[W_2(\rho_n,\pi)^2\le(1-mh)^nW_2(\rho_0,\pi)^2+hC.\]

The exact denominator is \(1-q=mh>0\).




Induction preserves probability and finite distance to the Gibbs law, allowing the previous one-step theorem to be applied at every time. If \(\delta\le hK^2/2\), the forcing comparison gives \(\mathcal F\le mh^2C\). Thus \(E_{n+1}\le(1-mh)E_n+mh^2C\), and its geometric floor is \((mh^2C)/(mh)=hC\). This establishes \[E_n\le(1-mh)^nE_0+hC.\] The cancellation is legitimate because \(m,h>0\); the resulting squared transport floor is linear in the step.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{splitting\_\allowbreak{}markov\_\allowbreak{}laws\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}bound}: Fully derived convergence for the actual iterated Gaussian splitting laws. The coefficient is uniform in the smoothing scale whenever the value gap is at most \texttt{h*K\ensuremath{{}^{2}}/\allowbreak{}2}.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Track the lifted particle coefficient}{Track the lifted particle coefficient}}


Let \(d=d_\theta+Nd_x\). In lifted coordinates substitute \(\bar m=Nm\), \(\bar L=NL_1\), \(\bar K=NK\), \(\sigma=\sqrt{2d}\), and

\[G_N=\sqrt{2(NL_1)^2d/(Nm)+2(NK)^2}+NK.\]

The definition \(C_N=C(Nm,NL_1,G_N,\sqrt{2d},NK)\) is exactly the transport coefficient. Its nonnegativity follows from \(N,m>0\) and the scalar sign lemma.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Reference\allowbreak{}G}: Smoothing-uniform moment bound in the scaled particle coordinates.
\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Transport\allowbreak{}Constant}: A finite explicit coefficient for the equal-step PIPGLA transport floor.
\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Transport\allowbreak{}Constant\_\allowbreak{}nonneg}: The explicit transport coefficient is nonnegative in the intended regime.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Identify actual equal-\allowbreak{}step PIPGLA laws}{Identify actual equal-step PIPGLA laws}}


For \(\lambda=\gamma>0\), set \(h=\gamma/N\), \(T=\operatorname{Pair}_{P_\gamma}\), and \(\pi=\Pi_{N,\gamma}\), the Gibbs target of \(g_1+g_2^\gamma\). The averaged proximal energy has the exact required coefficient, and the lifted value gap \(N\gamma K^2/2\) equals \(h(NK)^2/2\). The actual Gaussian algorithm law equals the canonical Markov law. Consequently

\[W_2(\rho_n,\Pi_{N,\gamma})^2\le(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\gamma})^2+\frac\gamma N C_N.\]

The coefficient \(C_N\) is independent of \(\gamma\); the reference target itself uses the scale \(\gamma\).




For the particle application substitute lifted modulus \(Nm\), smooth score constant \(NL_1\), time step \(h=\gamma/N\), dimension \(D=d_\theta+Nd_x\), and envelope scale \(\eta=\gamma\). The lifted score-magnitude bound is \(K_N=NK\) and the value gap is \(N\gamma K^2/2=hK_N^2/2\), exactly the required equal-step gap. The definitions \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Reference\allowbreak{}G} and \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Transport\allowbreak{}Constant} retain every resulting factor. The exact Gaussian-law identities transfer the Markov-law result to the supplied PIPGLA innovations, so the theorem concludes a rate for the implemented algorithm.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipgla\_\allowbreak{}equal\_\allowbreak{}step\_\allowbreak{}transport\_\allowbreak{}sq}: The actual PIPGLA Gaussian recursion, with equal proximal and forward steps, converges to within a proved \texttt{O(sqrt gamma)} transport radius of the true smooth reference Gibbs law. No invariant-law or convergence hypothesis is assumed.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Project splitting transport to the original parameter center}{Project splitting transport to the original parameter center}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingProjection-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Projection.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Let \(\rho_n\) be the actual PIPGLA law with any positive proximal parameter \(\lambda\), and compare it with the Gibbs target for \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\), \(\eta>0\). This module does not assume the algorithm already has a transport convergence rate.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Establish state and projected second moments}{Establish state and projected second moments}}


The law equals the pushforward of the constructed \(L^2\) chain. The map-measure criterion then proves \(z\mapsto z\in L^2(\rho_n)\). Apply the bounded linear parameter map and subtract a constant to get \(\theta(z)-a\in L^2(\rho_n)\) for every center \(a\).




The law equals the pushforward of \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Pipgla\allowbreak{}Chain}, so the identity observable under that law has a finite second moment. The continuous parameter projection preserves \(L^2\), and subtracting a constant center also preserves it because the law is a probability. These two declarations establish finiteness before the later comparison writes a real square-root integral.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}state\_\allowbreak{}mem\allowbreak{}Lp}: Every actual output law has a finite state second moment, without Gaussianity or a convergence assumption.
\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}mem\allowbreak{}Lp}: The actual parameter error has finite second moment about every center.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Combine transport, equilibrium concentration and center displacement}{Combine transport, equilibrium concentration and center displacement}}


Sharp marginal concentration gives target RMS at most \(\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}\), and the actual uniform potential gap gives \(\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|\le\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}\). Nonexpansive parameter projection and the center triangle inequality yield

\[\operatorname{RMS}_{\rho_n}(\theta_U)\le\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}+\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}+W_2(\rho_n,\Pi_{N,\eta}).\]

State and target moment bounds establish finite transport before the real-valued projection inequality is used.




Let \(\rho_n\) be the actual PIPGLA state law and \(\Pi_{N,\eta}\) the actual smoothed Gibbs target. Finite state moments make their Wasserstein distance finite. The nonexpansive parameter projection and sharp equilibrium concentration give \[R_{\theta_\eta}(\rho_n)\le W_2(\rho_n,\Pi_{N,\eta})+\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}.\] Then the center-change lemma and the proved marginal-center shift give \[R_{\theta_U}(\rho_n)\le W_2(\rho_n,\Pi_{N,\eta})+\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}+\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}.\] This intermediate statement retains the genuine transport distance; the later endpoint theorem supplies its numerical bound.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipgla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}original\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}transport}: A rigorous original-center comparison for the actual PIPGLA law. The remaining transport term is its genuine Wasserstein distance to the smoothed Gibbs target; this statement alone is not a convergence rate.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Sensitivity to the proximal parameter}{Sensitivity to the proximal parameter}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ProxParameter-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Parameter.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


For a convex bounded-below \(K\)-Lipschitz function, the actual minimizer satisfies \(\|P_\lambda(x)-P_\eta(x)\|\le K|\lambda-\eta|\). This feeds a pathwise comparison of PIPGLA chains with different positive proximal parameters.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Bounded supports imply value Lipschitzness}{Bounded supports imply value Lipschitzness}}


Choose a genuine subgradient \(s_x\) at each point with \(\|s_x\|\le K\). Its support at \(y\) gives \(f(x)-f(y)\le\langle s_x,x-y\rangle\le K\|x-y\|\). Reverse \(x,y\) to bound the absolute difference. This derives global Lipschitzness from a bounded section, without differentiability.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}bounded\_\allowbreak{}subgradients}: A bounded genuine subgradient at every input implies global Lipschitzness. In particular, the paper's uniformly bounded minimal section supplies this hypothesis, without adding a separate Lipschitz assumption to C1.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Compare two actual proximal minimizers}{Compare two actual proximal minimizers}}


Write \(q=P_\eta(x)\) and \(s=(x-q)/\eta\). Actual proximal optimality gives \(s\in\partial f(q)\) and \(\|s\|\le K\). The same support condition shows that \(q\) is also the \(\lambda\)-proximal point of the shifted input \(q+\lambda s\). Nonexpansiveness at scale \(\lambda\) therefore yields

\[\|P_\lambda(x)-q\|\le\|x-(q+\lambda s)\|=|\eta-\lambda|\|s\|\le K|\lambda-\eta|.\]

This proves the claimed sensitivity for every pair of positive scales.




Let \(q=P_\eta(x)\) and \(s=(x-q)/\eta\). The optimality theorem gives \(s\in\partial f(q)\), and global value Lipschitzness gives \(\|s\|\le K\). Reversing the optimality characterization shows that the same \(q\) is \(P_\lambda(q+\lambda s)\). Nonexpansiveness at parameter \(\lambda\) therefore gives \[\|P_\lambda(x)-P_\eta(x)\|\le\|x-(q+\lambda s)\|=|\eta-\lambda|\|s\|\le K|\lambda-\eta|.\] The local identities \texttt{hq}, \texttt{hxs} and \texttt{hid} implement exactly these substitutions; both parameters must be positive to cancel their residual denominators.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}sensitivity}: The actual proximal point changes by at most \texttt{K*|lam-\allowbreak{}eta|} when the positive smoothing parameter changes.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The averaged pair map preserves a uniform map difference}{The averaged pair map preserves a uniform map difference}}


Coordinate linearity proves \(\operatorname{Pair}_{P-Q}=\operatorname{Pair}_P-\operatorname{Pair}_Q\). If \(\sup_x\|P(x)-Q(x)\|\le c\), Jensen bounds the output square by the average of quantities at most \(c^2\), so the output norm is at most \(c\). Apply this with the single-pair proximal sensitivity to obtain the same \(K|\lambda-\eta|\) bound for the actual particle map.




Linearity in the supplied pair map gives \(\operatorname{pairwiseMap}(P-Q)=\operatorname{pairwiseMap}(P)-\operatorname{pairwiseMap}(Q)\). If \(\|P(x)-Q(x)\|\le c\) for all pairs, the averaged squared-distance inequality bounds the resulting state difference by \(N^{-1}\sum_i c^2=c^2\). Taking nonnegative square roots proves the same uniform \(c\), with no particle-number loss. Substitute \(c=K|\lambda-\eta|\) from the previous proximal sensitivity estimate.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map\_\allowbreak{}sub}: Pairwise averaging is linear in the supplied joint map.
\item \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map\_\allowbreak{}uniform\_\allowbreak{}difference\_\allowbreak{}le}: A uniform difference of joint maps stays uniformly bounded after the common-parameter averaging and latent-coordinate scaling.
\item \texttt{pairwise\_\allowbreak{}prox\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}sensitivity}: The proximal-parameter sensitivity has no particle-number loss.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Solve the pathwise parameter-\allowbreak{}perturbation recursion}{Solve the pathwise parameter-perturbation recursion}}


Insert an intermediate update with the same parameter, then combine shared-noise contraction and the uniform parameter difference:

\[e_{n+1}\le(1-m\gamma)e_n+K|\lambda-\eta|.\]

Induction yields \(e_n\le(1-m\gamma)^ne_0+K|\lambda-\eta|\sum_{j<n}(1-m\gamma)^j\). For \(\gamma>0\), the admissible contraction has \(0\le1-m\gamma<1\), so its sum is at most \(1/(m\gamma)\). These bounds hold for every supplied innovation path.




Insert an intermediate update with input \(w\) and proximal parameter \(\lambda\). The triangle inequality separates the state change from the parameter change, giving \[e_{k+1}\le(1-m\gamma)e_k+K|\lambda-\eta|.\] The finite-horizon theorem sums this derived pathwise recurrence, while \(\gamma>0\) gives the strict factor \(1-m\gamma<1\) needed for the floor \(K|\lambda-\eta|/(m\gamma)\). If the two runs start together, the initial term vanishes. No independence hypothesis is needed: both runs use the same arbitrary innovation sequence.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Update\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}comparison}: One step of two actual splitting schemes with different proximal parameters, the same time step, and the same supplied innovation.
\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Chain\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}comparison\_\allowbreak{}sum}: Exact finite-horizon comparison of two actual splitting recursions.
\item \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Chain\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}comparison\_\allowbreak{}bound}: The resulting pathwise difference floor is valid for any two positive proximal parameters; it is not assumed to be an algorithm error bound.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A proximal corridor gives an explicit scalar bound}{A proximal corridor gives an explicit scalar bound}}


If \(0<m\gamma<1\) and \(\gamma\le\lambda\le\gamma/(1-m\gamma)\), then \(|\lambda-\gamma|=\lambda-\gamma\le m\gamma^2/(1-m\gamma)\). Divide by the positive \(m\gamma\) and multiply by \(K\) to get \(K|\lambda-\gamma|/(m\gamma)\le K\gamma/(1-m\gamma)\). This is a scalar consequence of the true sensitivity theorem.




In the corridor \(\gamma\le\lambda\le\gamma/(1-m\gamma)\), positivity of \(1-m\gamma\) gives \[0\le\lambda-\gamma\le\frac{m\gamma^2}{1-m\gamma}.\] Therefore \(K|\lambda-\gamma|/(m\gamma)\le K\gamma/(1-m\gamma)\). The Lean proof first removes the absolute value using the lower corridor inequality, then clears the two positive denominators. This term measures the additional error incurred by changing the proximal parameter away from the equal-step algorithm.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}corridor\_\allowbreak{}floor}: In the paper's proximal-parameter corridor, the additional comparison floor against \texttt{eta=gamma} is of order \texttt{gamma}, with every denominator displayed.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{PIPGLA about the original center for every positive proximal scale}{PIPGLA about the original center for every positive proximal scale}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingTheorem-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Splitting\allowbreak{}Theorem.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Assume the same strongly convex smooth \(g_1\), convex bounded-below \(K\)-Lipschitz \(g_2\), \(N>0\), \(0<m\le L_1\), \(\lambda,\gamma>0\), and \(\gamma\le2/(m+L_1)\). Initialization is in \(L^2\) and the actual coordinate Gaussian innovations satisfy joint independence.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Convert the pathwise scale comparison into RMS}{Convert the pathwise scale comparison into RMS}}


Drive the \(\lambda\)- and \(\eta\)-chains by the same initial variable and innovations. Their state distance is at most \(K|\lambda-\eta|/(m\gamma)\). Both chains have finite second moments. The parameter projection is nonexpansive, so Minkowski gives

\[\operatorname{RMS}_\lambda(a)\le\operatorname{RMS}_\eta(a)+\frac{K|\lambda-\eta|}{m\gamma}.\]

The proof works with the actual \(L^2\) realizations and then identifies their pushforward integrals.




Here is the conversion from a pathwise comparison to the two displayed law integrals. On the supplied probability space let \(X\) and \(Y\) be the actual \(L^2\) PIPGLA iterates with proximal parameters \(\lambda\) and \(\eta\), the same initialization, and the same innovations. The pathwise parameter-sensitivity theorem gives

\[\|X(\omega)-Y(\omega)\|\le c:=\frac{K|\lambda-\eta|}{m\gamma}\quad\text{almost surely}.\]

The transient term vanishes because both initial states agree. The full-measure representative equalities for the two chains and their subtraction justify this substitution in \texttt{hpoint}. Since \(Q\) has total mass one, integrating the uniform bound gives \(\|X-Y\|_{L^2}\le c\); this is \texttt{h\allowbreak{}XY}.



Define \(A=P_\theta X-\theta\) and \(B=P_\theta Y-\theta\) as \(L^2\) variables. Linearity gives \(A-B=P_\theta(X-Y)\), and the projection norm bound gives \(\|A-B\|_2\le\|X-Y\|_2\). Thus \(\|A\|_2\le\|B\|_2+c\), the inequality \texttt{hn}. Finally, \texttt{hnorm} first identifies each actual law as the pushforward of its \(L^2\) iterate, then changes variables in the quadratic integral and uses \(\sqrt{\|A\|_2^2}=\|A\|_2\). All these norms are finite by construction. Substituting those two identities into \texttt{hn} proves the stated RMS comparison.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipgla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}prox\_\allowbreak{}comparison}: Actual PIPGLA parameter RMS values at two positive proximal parameters are separated by the proved pathwise parameter-sensitivity floor.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Combine equal-\allowbreak{}step transport and original-\allowbreak{}center projection}{Combine equal-step transport and original-center projection}}


Set the comparison scale to \(\eta=\gamma\). The equal-step law theorem and the original-center projection inequality give

\[\begin{aligned}\operatorname{RMS}_\lambda(\theta_U)\le{}&\frac{K|\lambda-\gamma|}{m\gamma}+\sqrt{\frac{\gamma K^2}{m}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}\\&+\sqrt{(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\gamma})^2+\frac\gamma N C_N}.\end{aligned}\]

Here \(C_N\) is the explicit finite coefficient from the endpoint module. It is independent of the time step. Every positive \(\lambda\) is covered, with the displayed parameter-comparison cost; a vanishing error as \(\gamma\to0\) additionally requires controlling that cost. This is the proved replacement rate, not the paper’s printed heat-flow constants.




The equal-step transport theorem controls the actual law at proximal scale \(\gamma\) (with the lifted smoothing scale prescribed in its statement). The projection theorem adds the sharp equilibrium RMS and the original-center displacement. To handle a different positive proximal parameter \(\lambda\), compare the two pointwise runs under the same innovations: their uniform difference is at most \(K|\lambda-\gamma|/(m\gamma)\). Integrating this deterministic bound yields the same RMS difference because the probability has mass one. The final result adds this scale-comparison term to the already proved equal-step error; the parameter corridor then supplies its explicit simpler upper bound.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipgla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}original}: A genuine finite-time original-center theorem for PIPGLA, for any positive proximal parameter. In the paper's corridor the additional sensitivity term is \texttt{O(gamma)}; the derived transport and center-bias terms are \texttt{O(sqrt gamma)}. All stationarity, moment, Brownian, and law-recurrence obligations are proved in the imported endpoints rather than assumed here.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Gaussian displacement and frozen-\allowbreak{}gradient error energy}{Gaussian displacement and frozen-gradient error energy}}
\label{proof-module-UnadjustedLangevin-LSI-GaussianDrift-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Unadjusted\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}LSI/\allowbreak{}Gaussian\allowbreak{}Drift.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


For \(Y_t=X-t\nabla U(X)+\sqrt{2t}Z\), independent Gaussian noise makes the cross term vanish, giving \(\mathbb E\|Y_t-X\|^2=t^2\mathbb E\|\nabla U(X)\|^2+2td\). Gradient Lipschitzness bounds the corresponding frozen-gradient error by \(L^2\) times this moment.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{integral norm sq std\allowbreak{}Gaussian}{integral norm sq stdGaussian}}


Choose a finite orthonormal basis and use \(\|Z\|^2=\sum_i\langle e_i,Z\rangle^2\). Each standard Gaussian coordinate has second moment one, proved using its dual variance formula and zero mean. Gaussian \(L^2\) membership justifies integrating the finite sum, giving \(\mathbb E\|Z\|^2=d\), also when \(d=0\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{integral norm sq affine std\allowbreak{}Gaussian}{integral norm sq affine stdGaussian}}


Expand \(\|ag(X)+bZ\|^2=a^2\|g(X)\|^2+2ab\langle g(X),Z\rangle+b^2\|Z\|^2\). The \(L^2\) hypothesis on \(g\) and Gaussian \(L^2\) membership prove all three terms integrable on the independent product.



The product bilinear-integral theorem factors the cross expectation as \(\langle\mathbb Eg(X),\mathbb EZ\rangle=0\). The product marginals identify the first energy, and \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}std\allowbreak{}Gaussian} identifies the noise energy. Hence the integral is \(a^2\mathbb E\|g\|^2+b^2d\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{mala\allowbreak{}Proposal sub initial}{malaProposal sub initial}}


Subtract \(x\) from \(T_t(x,z)=x-t\nabla U(x)+\sqrt{2t}z\). Additive cancellation leaves \(-t\nabla U(x)+\sqrt{2t}z\). This algebraic identity shows why a state second moment is unnecessary when only displacement energy is estimated.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{frozen displacement second moment}{frozen displacement second moment}}


Apply the affine Gaussian energy formula with \(a=-t\), \(b=\sqrt{2t}\), and \(g=\nabla U\). For \(t\ge0\), \(b^2=2t\), yielding \(\mathbb E\|T_t-X\|^2=t^2\mathbb E\|\nabla U(X)\|^2+2td\). Independence and centering have already discharged the cross term.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{frozen gradient error energy}{frozen gradient error energy}}


Lipschitzness gives \(\|\nabla U(T_t)-\nabla U(X)\|^2\le L^2\|T_t-X\|^2\) pointwise. Convert integrability of the squared gradient to \(L^2\) membership, so the displacement has an integrable squared norm.



Continuity of the proposal and gradient proves measurability of the squared discrepancy. Domination by the integrable displacement energy proves its integrability; \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}mono} and the exact displacement moment then give \(L^2(t^2G_0+2td)\). This is a joint-law estimate; a state-space conditional drift is constructed separately.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{frozen displacement mem\allowbreak{}Lp}{frozen displacement memLp}}


Pull the square-integrable initial gradient to the first product coordinate and the square-integrable Gaussian identity to the second. Their scalar multiples and sum lie in \(L^2\). The displacement identity rewrites this sum as \(T_t(X,Z)-X\).

\section{\texorpdfstring{Orthogonal masks and the exact removed-\allowbreak{}noise cost}{Orthogonal masks and the exact removed-noise cost}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDNoise-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}MYPGDNoise.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Let \(P_\theta\) retain the common parameter block of the scaled particle state, and \(P_x=I-P_\theta\) retain the latent blocks. For a standard Gaussian vector \(\xi\), the removed parameter noise has mean zero and second moment \(d_\theta\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Define the actual complementary coordinate projections}{Define the actual complementary coordinate projections}}


Set \(P_\theta(\theta,x)=(\theta,0)\) and \(P_x(\theta,x)=(0,x)\). Coordinate evaluation proves the four block identities by unfolding the definitions. These are bounded linear maps, so masking an \(L^2\) innovation remains an actual \(L^2\) random variable.




Denote the two maps by \(P_\theta\) and \(P_x=I-P_\theta\). On a scaled particle state, \(P_\theta(\theta,z_1,\ldots,z_N)=(\theta,0,\ldots,0)\), while \(P_x(\theta,z_1,\ldots,z_N)=(0,z_1,\ldots,z_N)\). The four coordinate lemmas establish these formulas directly; the subtraction defining \texttt{latent\allowbreak{}Noise\allowbreak{}CLM} is an equality of continuous linear maps, so all later compositions are measurable.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{parameter\allowbreak{}Noise\allowbreak{}CLM}: Retain the parameter coordinates and erase the latent coordinates.
\item \texttt{latent\allowbreak{}Noise\allowbreak{}CLM}: Retain precisely the latent noise of the original particle scheme.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Compute the projection geometry and Gaussian moments}{Compute the projection geometry and Gaussian moments}}


The Euclidean square is \(\|P_\theta z\|^2=\sum_{j=1}^{d_\theta}z_j^2\), hence \(\|P_\theta z\|\le\|z\|\). A bounded linear map commutes with the Gaussian expectation, giving \(\mathbb E P_\theta\xi=0\). Each retained standard coordinate has second moment one; finite summation gives \(\mathbb E\|P_\theta\xi\|^2=d_\theta\).




The squared norm expands to \(\sum_{j=1}^{d_\theta}z_j^2\), with no latent terms. Every coordinate square is integrable because the standard Gaussian identity map belongs to \(L^2\). The coordinate variance identity gives \(\mathbb E z_j^2=1\) and the continuous-linear-map integration identity gives \(\mathbb E P_\theta Z=P_\theta\mathbb EZ=0\). Interchanging only a finite sum and the integral yields \(\mathbb E\|P_\theta Z\|^2=d_\theta\). No factor \(N\) belongs in this dimension count.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The product law cancels the mixed term exactly}{The product law cancels the mixed term exactly}}


For any square-integrable \(g\) on a probability space independent of \(\xi\), expand \(\|g+bP_\theta\xi\|^2\). Fubini and bilinearity factor its mixed expectation as \(\langle\mathbb E g,\mathbb E P_\theta\xi\rangle=0\). Thus

\[\mathbb E\|g+bP_\theta\xi\|^2=\mathbb E\|g\|^2+b^2d_\theta.\]

The proof establishes integrability of every term before distributing the integral.




For a general \(g\in L^2(\mu)\), use the actual product law \(\mu\otimes\mathcal N(0,I)\), and expand \[\|g(x)+bP_\theta z\|^2=\|g(x)\|^2+2b\langle g(x),P_\theta z\rangle+b^2\|P_\theta z\|^2.\] The facts \texttt{hgsq} and \texttt{hzsq} prove integrability of the two squares on the product; \texttt{hcross} proves integrability of the bilinear term, so splitting the integral is justified. Independence here is the product-measure construction itself: \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}prod\_\allowbreak{}bilin} factors the mixed integral as \(\langle\mathbb Eg,\mathbb E P_\theta Z\rangle=0\). Hence \(\mathbb E\|g+bP_\theta Z\|^2=\mathbb E\|g\|^2+b^2d_\theta\), with no assumption that \(g\) is centered.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}parameter\allowbreak{}Noise\_\allowbreak{}sq}: Exact independent-noise identity, for an arbitrary square-integrable source. The mixed term vanishes by the actual product Gaussian law.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{A concrete latent/\allowbreak{}full Gaussian transition coupling}{A concrete latent/full Gaussian transition coupling}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDTransport-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}MYPGDTransport.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


For an \(r\)-Lipschitz deterministic map \(D\), compare \(D(x)+aP_x\xi\) and \(D(y)+a\xi\) using the same fresh standard Gaussian vector. Initial laws are probability measures with finite second moments.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Lipschitz maps and explicit pairs have finite transport cost}{Lipschitz maps and explicit pairs have finite transport cost}}


Center \(D\) at zero to use \(\|D(x)-D(0)\|\le r\|x\|\), then add back its constant value; this proves preservation of \(L^2\). For two \(L^2\) variables on one space, their joint law is a coupling. Its cost bounds \(W_2\), yielding both finiteness and \(W_2(\mathcal L(X),\mathcal L(Y))^2\le\mathbb E\|X-Y\|^2\).




A globally Lipschitz map \(D\) need not vanish at zero. The first lemma writes \(D(X)=[D(X)-D(0)]+D(0)\): the centered map preserves \(L^2\), and the constant belongs to \(L^2\) because the measure is a probability. For the transport lemma, convert \(X,Y\) to their \(L^2\) classes. Their joint realization is an admissible coupling of their pushforwards, so \[W_2(X_\#P,Y_\#P)^2\le\|X-Y\|_{L^2(P)}^2=\int\|X-Y\|^2dP.\] The representative equalities \texttt{hx}, \texttt{hy} prove that these pushforwards are the original laws, and the finite norm bound proves finite Wasserstein distance before squaring its real value.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mem\allowbreak{}Lp\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}comp}: Globally Lipschitz maps preserve finite quadratic moments on a probability space.
\item \texttt{wasserstein\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}integral\_\allowbreak{}pair}: Direct finite-cost transport estimate for an actual L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} pair.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Measurable Gaussian maps preserve second moments}{Measurable Gaussian maps preserve second moments}}


The two maps are \(T_x(u,\xi)=D(u)+aP_x\xi\) and \(T(u,\xi)=D(u)+a\xi\). Lipschitz continuity of \(D\), linearity of \(P_x\), addition and scalar multiplication prove joint measurability. On \(\mu\otimes\mathcal N(0,I)\), each summand belongs to \(L^2\), so both pushforward transition laws have finite second moments.




Both updates use a fresh \(Z\sim\mathcal N(0,I)\), independent of the input: \(D(x)+aP_xZ\) and \(D(x)+aZ\). Their measurability follows by composing continuous \(D\), the coordinate projections, addition and scalar multiplication. For moment propagation, \texttt{comp\_\allowbreak{}fst} pulls the \(L^2\) drift onto the input–Gaussian product and \texttt{comp\_\allowbreak{}snd} pulls the Gaussian noise onto that same space. The sum is in \(L^2\); \texttt{mem\allowbreak{}Lp\_\allowbreak{}map\_\allowbreak{}measure\_\allowbreak{}iff} then transfers this fact to the output law. Thus the next coupling argument has actual finite-moment marginals.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{latent\allowbreak{}Gaussian\allowbreak{}Update}: The deterministic map followed by latent-only Gaussian noise.
\item \texttt{full\allowbreak{}Gaussian\allowbreak{}Update}: The same map followed by full Gaussian noise.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Extend every input coupling by an independent Gaussian}{Extend every input coupling by an independent Gaussian}}


For any input coupling \(c\) of \(\mu,\nu\), work on \(c\otimes\mathcal N(0,I)\). The two outputs have precisely the required Markov marginals, and their difference is \(D(x)-D(y)-aP_\theta\xi\). The exact removed-noise identity gives squared coupling cost \(\mathbb E_c\|D(x)-D(y)\|^2+a^2d_\theta\), bounded by \(r^2\operatorname{cost}(c)^2+a^2d_\theta\). This constructs the recurrence input, with no optimal-coupling assumption.




Start from any input coupling \(c\) of \(\mu,\nu\), and work on \(c\otimes\mathcal N(0,I)\). Define \(F=D(X)+aP_xZ\) and \(H=D(Y)+aZ\). The facts \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Flaw} and \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Hlaw} check separately that their marginals are the desired two Markov transitions; this ensures the constructed pair is a coupling of the actual algorithms.



The cancellation in \texttt{hid} is \[F-H=D(X)-D(Y)-aP_\theta Z.\] By the product Gaussian identity, its mean square is \(\mathbb E_c\|D(X)-D(Y)\|^2+a^2d_\theta\). If \(D\) is \(r\)-Lipschitz, the first term is bounded by \(r^2\mathbb E_c\|X-Y\|^2\). Finally \texttt{hnorm} identifies the latter integral with the square of the coupling cost. All three random variables are proved square integrable before any of these integral identities is used.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{latent\_\allowbreak{}full\_\allowbreak{}markov\_\allowbreak{}coupling\_\allowbreak{}sq}: Actual latent-noise and full-noise one-step laws admit the stated squared coupling bound. The independent centered parameter noise supplies exactly \texttt{a\ensuremath{{}^{2}}*dtheta}, and has no mixed contribution.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Iterated finite-\allowbreak{}particle noise-\allowbreak{}removal comparison}{Iterated finite-particle noise-removal comparison}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGD-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}MYPGD.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Finite-moment latent-only and full Gaussian chains use the same deterministic \(r\)-contractive map \(D\) and amplitude \(a\). Their actual Markov laws are compared at every iteration.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Pass from every input coupling to actual transport}{Pass from every input coupling to actual transport}}


The previous module supplies \(W_2(T_x\mu,T\nu)^2\le r^2\operatorname{cost}(c)^2+a^2d_\theta\) for every finite input coupling. Finite second moments prove \(W_2(\mu,\nu)<\infty\), so the infimum lemma yields \(W_2(T_x\mu,T\nu)^2\le r^2W_2(\mu,\nu)^2+a^2d_\theta\).




The preceding estimate holds for every finite-cost input coupling, with the same additive \(a^2d_\theta\). Finite second moments first ensure \(W_2(\mu,\nu)<\infty\). The infimum lemma then gives \[W_2(\mu P_x,\nu P)^2\le r^2W_2(\mu,\nu)^2+a^2d_\theta.\] The existence of one finite-cost coupling also proves finiteness of the output distance. An optimal coupling is unnecessary: the argument uses a common bound for the entire family of admissible couplings.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{latent\_\allowbreak{}full\_\allowbreak{}markov\_\allowbreak{}sq}: The finite-moment independent Gaussian transition satisfies the derived squared transport recurrence.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Iterate from a common initialization}{Iterate from a common initialization}}


Measurability preserves probability mass, and the one-step moment lemmas preserve \(L^2\) by induction. Since both chains start from \(\mu\), their initial transport distance is zero. For \(0\le r<1\), the geometric recurrence gives \(W_2(\rho_n^x,\rho_n)^2\le a^2d_\theta/(1-r^2)\), along with finiteness at every time.




Let \(E_k=W_2(\mu_k^x,\mu_k^{\mathrm{full}})^2\), with the same initial law. The two inductions \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Mm} and \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Im} propagate finite second moments, while \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Mp} and \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Ip} propagate probability mass. Therefore the one-step theorem applies at every \(k\), giving \(E_{k+1}\le r^2E_k+a^2d_\theta\). A diagonal realization proves \(E_0=0\). Since \(0\le r<1\), the affine-recurrence sum yields \[E_k\le a^2d_\theta\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}r^{2j}\le\frac{a^2d_\theta}{1-r^2}.\] This recurrence is derived from the transitions, rather than a hypothesis of this theorem.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{latent\_\allowbreak{}full\_\allowbreak{}markov\_\allowbreak{}laws\_\allowbreak{}sq}: The actual iterated independent-Gaussian laws differ by at most the explicit noise-removal floor. No error recurrence is assumed.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The genuine smoothed forward map contracts}{The genuine smoothed forward map contracts}}


The common deterministic smoothed step contracts by \(r=1-m\gamma\) when \(0<m\le L_\eta\) and \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\). Couple any two current laws, share their latent Gaussian noise, and use the same full Gaussian vector for the MYIPLA step. The exact noise identity proves the actual one-step squared transport estimate


\[
W_2(\rho^{\mathrm{PGD}}_{k+1},\rho^{\mathrm{IPLA}}_{k+1})^2
 \le r^2 W_2(\rho^{\mathrm{PGD}}_k,\rho^{\mathrm{IPLA}}_k)^2
       +\frac{2\gamma d_\theta}{N}.
\]


The proof constructs each output coupling, establishes its finite moments, and takes the infimum over input couplings. Starting from the same initial law, \(0\le r<1\) and \(1-r^2=m\gamma(2-m\gamma)\ge m\gamma\) give


\[
W_2(\rho^{\mathrm{PGD}}_n,\rho^{\mathrm{IPLA}}_n)^2
 \le\frac{2d_\theta}{Nm}.
\]


This is a conservative comparison radius, not a claim that removing parameter noise worsens the algorithm. The estimate follows from the two actual recursions and their genuine independent Gaussian transitions.




For \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\), the earlier Moreau results give differentiability, strong convexity \(m\) and score Lipschitz constant \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\). Lifting gives constants \(Nm,NL_\eta\), and the Euler step \(\gamma/N\) consequently contracts with \(r=1-m\gamma\). The admissible step \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\), with \(m\le L_\eta\), proves \(0\le m\gamma\le1\), hence \(0\le r<1\).



Substitute \(a^2=2\gamma/N\) into the preceding floor. The scalar fact \texttt{hbound} is \[1-(1-m\gamma)^2=m\gamma(2-m\gamma)\ge m\gamma.\] Its denominator is positive, so \[\frac{(2\gamma/N)d_\theta}{1-(1-m\gamma)^2}\le\frac{2d_\theta}{Nm}.\] The proof controls removal of parameter noise in a finite-particle algorithm; it does not replace this floor with a mean-field limit assertion.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mypgd\allowbreak{}Drift\allowbreak{}Map}: The smooth Euler map used by both MYPGD and MYIPLA.
\item \texttt{mypgd\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}markov\_\allowbreak{}transport\_\allowbreak{}sq}: Genuine MYPGD/\allowbreak{}MYIPLA comparison with a uniform finite-particle floor. This bound concerns the actual Gaussian Markov transitions. It is a proved replacement for, rather than an assertion of, the source's mean-field estimate.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{MYPGD pointwise recursion and its exact Markov law}{MYPGD pointwise recursion and its exact Markov law}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDAlgorithm-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}MYPGDAlgorithm.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


MYPGD is the expanded MYIPLA drift with only latent Gaussian noise retained. The source definitions use actual pairwise proximity and independent smoothing and time parameters.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Mask only the innovations in the pointwise chain}{Mask only the innovations in the pointwise chain}}


Define the update by replacing \(\xi\) in MYIPLA with \(P_x\xi\). The zero and successor recursion identities show inductively that the resulting MYPGD chain equals the MYIPLA pointwise chain driven by \((P_x\xi_k)_k\), and equals the generic noise-driven chain for its own update. Its law is the pushforward of this actual chain.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mypgd\allowbreak{}Update}: MYPGD removes precisely the parameter noise from the displayed MYIPLA step.
\item \texttt{mypgd\allowbreak{}Chain}: The actual MYPGD chain driven by supplied full innovations.
\item \texttt{mypgd\allowbreak{}Chain\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}myipla\allowbreak{}Chain}: MYPGD is exactly MYIPLA applied to the latent-only innovations.
\item \texttt{mypgd\allowbreak{}Laws}: The output law of the actual MYPGD recursion.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Recognize the shared deterministic map}{Recognize the shared deterministic map}}


Let \(F_\eta=\sum_i(g_1+g_2^\eta)(\theta,x_i)\) in scaled coordinates and \(h=\gamma/N\). The displayed MYPGD step is exactly


\[
X_{k+1}=X_k-h\nabla F_\eta(X_k)+\sqrt{2h}\,P_x\xi_k.
\]


Its common parameter coordinate has no Gaussian perturbation; each physical latent coordinate has the usual \(\sqrt{2\gamma}\) noise. The expanded proximal-residual formula is identified with this gradient step. The pointwise chain is then identified with its measurable Gaussian Markov recursion.



Initialization has finite second moment, and the standard Gaussian vectors are independent across coordinates and times and independent of initialization. The parameter coordinates of those vectors are erased in MYPGD. They remain available as auxiliary noise for the comparison with MYIPLA. No initial iid-particle hypothesis is needed for this weaker finite-particle theorem.



The auxiliary measurability estimate uses the unconditional bound \(\operatorname{Lip}(I-(\gamma/N)\nabla F_\eta)\le1+|\gamma/N|\,NL_\eta\). It needs no contraction restriction and supplies measurable updates even before the convergence regime is imposed.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mypgd\allowbreak{}Update\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}latent\allowbreak{}Gaussian\allowbreak{}Update}: The drift/\allowbreak{}noise formula for the actual Moreau proximal construction.
\item \texttt{myipla\allowbreak{}Update\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}full\allowbreak{}Gaussian\allowbreak{}Update}: The full-noise update used for comparison is the actual MYIPLA update.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Independent coordinate Gaussians give the Markov recursion}{Independent coordinate Gaussians give the Markov recursion}}


The Gaussian-innovation hypotheses imply that vector innovations have standard Gaussian law, are independent over time, and are independent of initialization. The measurable-chain law theorem therefore identifies the two pushforwards with the actual iterated Markov transitions \(D+aP_x\xi\) and \(D+a\xi\). This transfers coupling estimates from the canonical product space to any admissible algorithm realization.




The two update identities recognize the same deterministic map \(D(z)=z-(\gamma/N)\nabla V_{N,\eta}(z)\). MYPGD adds \(\sqrt{2\gamma/N}P_x\xi\), while MYIPLA adds the full \(\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\xi\). The coordinate-Gaussian assumptions give the complete independent standard vector-noise path and independence from initialization. The shared iid-chain law theorem consequently identifies each supplied implementation with its iterated Markov transition. This equality is needed before the abstract Markov comparison can be cited for these algorithms.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mypgd\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}markov\allowbreak{}Laws}: The actual Gaussian realization has the proved latent-noise Markov law.
\item \texttt{myipla\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}markov\allowbreak{}Laws}: The same full innovations give the ordinary full-noise MYIPLA Markov law.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Actual MYPGD bound about the original marginal center}{Actual MYPGD bound about the original marginal center}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDTheorem-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}MYPGDTheorem.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Under the MYIPLA primitive assumptions, also assume \(m\le L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\). The algorithm uses genuine latent-only Gaussian updates; \(N>0\), \(m,\eta,\gamma>0\), and \(\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Identify actual algorithm laws in the comparison}{Identify actual algorithm laws in the comparison}}


Rewrite both output laws as the Gaussian Markov recursions of the shared smoothed drift. The initialization pushforward has finite second moment because its representative belongs to \(L^2\). The iterated noise-removal theorem now proves finite transport distance and \(W_2(\rho_n^{\rm PGD},\rho_n^{\rm IPLA})^2\le2d_\theta/(Nm)\).




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mypgd\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}actual\_\allowbreak{}transport\_\allowbreak{}sq}: The actual MYPGD and MYIPLA laws have the proved finite transport bound. Their common full Gaussian input is used only to identify the two different Markov transitions; the MYPGD output has latent-only noise.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Transfer parameter RMS through a nonexpansive projection}{Transfer parameter RMS through a nonexpansive projection}}


The parameter map has operator norm at most one. The projected RMS transport inequality yields \(\operatorname{RMS}_{\rm PGD}(\theta)\le W_2(\rho_n^{\rm PGD},\rho_n^{\rm IPLA})+\operatorname{RMS}_{\rm IPLA}(\theta)\). MYIPLA finite moments and the finite comparison imply the MYPGD moment is finite before passing to real square-root integrals. Taking the square root of the comparison gives the added radius \(\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\).




Finite second moments of both algorithm laws give finite transport. Projecting a coupling onto the common parameter, whose operator norm is at most one, gives \[R_{\mathrm{MYPGD}}(\theta_*)\le R_{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}(\theta_*)+W_2(\rho_n^{\mathrm{MYPGD}},\rho_n^{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}).\] The actual-law comparison bounds the squared last term by \(2d_\theta/(Nm)\); nonnegative square roots therefore bound it by \(\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\). The final theorem inserts the already proved original-center MYIPLA estimate, retaining its smoothing, equilibrium and discretization contributions.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mypgd\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}myipla}: Any parameter RMS for actual MYPGD is bounded by the corresponding actual MYIPLA RMS plus an explicit finite-particle noise-removal radius.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Insert the proved original-\allowbreak{}center MYIPLA theorem}{Insert the proved original-center MYIPLA theorem}}


Apply the parameter-projection transport inequality to the two actual laws, then use the proved MYIPLA original-center theorem. With \(L_\eta=L_1+1/\eta\) and the assumptions above,


\[
\begin{aligned}
\left(\mathbb E\|\theta^{\mathrm{PGD}}_n-\theta_\star\|^2\right)^{1/2}
\le{}&\sqrt{\frac{2d_\theta}{Nm}}
 +\sqrt{\frac{\eta K^2}{m}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}\\
&+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\eta})\\
&+\frac{33}{20}\frac{L_\eta}{m}
 \sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}{N}}\sqrt\gamma.
\end{aligned}
\]


Every term is finite under the primitive assumptions, and every law in the statement is the actual defined algorithm law. This is a genuine finite-particle replacement for Appendix B’s Theorem B.1. It has a Gibbs initial-law transient and an explicit conservative comparison term; it does not reproduce the paper’s mean-field metric, constants, or stronger Taylor smoothing expression.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}mypgd\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}original}: Fully proved finite-time original-center control for the actual MYPGD Gaussian recursion. The extra \texttt{sqrt (2*dtheta/\allowbreak{}(N*m))} is a conservative derived comparison radius, not the source's unformalized mean-field constant.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{The actual mean-\allowbreak{}field force is Lipschitz in W\ensuremath{{}_{2}}}{The actual mean-field force is Lipschitz in W₂}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldDrift-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Drift.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


For a pair potential \(V\) with \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient, define \(b_V(\theta,\mu)=\int\nabla_\theta V(\theta,x)\mu(dx)\). The measures are probability laws with finite second moments. Every Bochner expectation and every finite real transport distance is justified.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A Lipschitz observable preserves L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} and controls expectation differences}{A Lipschitz observable preserves L² and controls expectation differences}}


Center an observable \(\phi\) at \(\phi(0)\); its norm growth is bounded by \(K\|x\|\), so \(\phi(X)\in L^2\subset L^1\). Given any finite-cost coupling \(c\) of \(\mu,\nu\), pushforward integration and Cauchy–Schwarz give

\[\left\|\int\phi\,d\mu-\int\phi\,d\nu\right\|\le K\left(\int\|x-y\|^2dc\right)^{1/2}.\]

Taking the actual coupling infimum yields the bound \(K W_2(\mu,\nu)\), with finiteness supplied by the second moments.




The nonzero value \(\varphi(0)\) must be retained when proving integrability. Write \(\varphi(X)=[\varphi(X)-\varphi(0)]+\varphi(0)\). The first term is in \(L^2\) because its Lipschitz map vanishes at zero; the second is a constant in \(L^2\) on a probability space. This proves \texttt{mem\allowbreak{}Lp\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}observable}, including the offset.



For the transport estimate, take any coupling \(c\) of \(\mu,\nu\). Its marginal identities pull the given second moments onto its two coordinate random variables. Applying the observable lemma to both coordinates proves their \(L^2\) membership and hence their Bochner integrability. The fact \texttt{hid} then changes variables using those marginals and combines the two integrals:

\[\int\varphi\,d\mu-\int\varphi\,d\nu=\int[\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)]\,dc(x,y).\]

The successive inequalities in \texttt{hfamily} are the integral triangle inequality, the probability-space comparison \(\|F\|_1\le\|F\|_2\), and the pointwise Lipschitz bound \(\|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\|\le K\|x-y\|\). They give the same upper bound \(K\,\operatorname{cost}(c)\) for every coupling. Taking the infimum in the extended nonnegative reals proves \texttt{hbound}. Finite input second moments already imply finite \(W_2\), so the product \(KW_2\) is finite and the final conversion to real numbers preserves the inequality.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mem\allowbreak{}Lp\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}observable}: A Lipschitz observable with a nonzero offset still preserves finite second moments on a probability space.
\item \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}wasserstein2}: Actual expectations of a Lipschitz observable are Lipschitz in W\ensuremath{{}_{2}}. Both input probability laws have finite second moment; infinity is never removed by an unguarded real conversion.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Project the true joint gradient to its parameter block}{Project the true joint gradient to its parameter block}}


The bounded linear map \(P_\theta\) keeps the parameter coordinates. Its squared norm is a sub-sum of the full Euclidean square, hence \(\|P_\theta z\|\le\|z\|\). Define \(\phi_\theta(x)=P_\theta\nabla V(\theta,x)\), then define the mean-field force as its actual Bochner integral. These definitions use the existing joint potential, with no arbitrary force field substituted for its gradient.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{joint\allowbreak{}Parameter\allowbreak{}CLM}: The parameter block of a parameter/\allowbreak{}latent joint vector.
