\item \texttt{parameter\allowbreak{}Gradient}: The parameter component of the actual joint gradient.
\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Drift}: The actual averaged parameter drift, as a Bochner integral.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Control joint parameter and latent perturbations}{Control joint parameter and latent perturbations}}


Decompose a joint pair difference into its two coordinate inclusions, giving \(\|(\theta,x)-(\theta',x')\|\le\|\theta-\theta'\|+\|x-x'\|\). Gradient Lipschitzness and the nonexpansive parameter projection imply

\[\|\phi_\theta(x)-\phi_{\theta'}(x')\|\le L(\|\theta-\theta'\|+\|x-x'\|).\]

Fixing \(\theta=\theta'\) proves that the latent observable itself is \(L\)-Lipschitz.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Separate the parameter change from the law change}{Separate the parameter change from the law change}}


For a differentiable potential \(V\), define


\[
b_V(\theta,\mu)=\int \nabla_\theta V(\theta,x)\,\mu(dx).
\]


Assume \(\nabla V\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz and \(\mu,\nu\) are probability laws with finite second moment. Coordinate projection has norm at most one, so the integrand is Lipschitz and grows at most linearly. Its Bochner expectation is therefore integrable. For any finite-cost coupling, compare its two integrands, apply the norm-of-integral inequality, and use Cauchy–Schwarz. Taking the infimum of actual coupling costs gives


\[
\|b_V(\theta,\mu)-b_V(\theta',\nu)\|
 \le L\bigl(\|\theta-\theta'\|+W_2(\mu,\nu)\bigr).
\]


With the genuine split envelope \(V=g_1+g_2^\eta\), the proved constant is \(L_1+1/\eta\). The paper’s \(1/\eta\) constant applies to a full envelope, not to this split expression in general. This is a corrected \textbf{W\ensuremath{{}_{2}} version} of Lemma C.1: its right-hand side is weaker than the paper’s W\ensuremath{{}_{1}} statement, and the distinction is explicit. Propositions C.2–C.3 are not consequences formalized by this lemma alone.




Fix an arbitrary coupling \((X,Y)\) of the two latent laws. Split the force difference into a parameter change at fixed law and a law change at fixed parameter. Nonexpansive parameter projection and the full score's \(L\)-Lipschitz property give \(\|\nabla_\theta U(\theta,x)-\nabla_\theta U(\vartheta,y)\|\le L(\|\theta-\vartheta\|+\|x-y\|)\). Integrability of this observable follows from the latent second moments. Integrate, use Cauchy–Schwarz on \(\mathbb E\|X-Y\|\), and take the coupling infimum to obtain \[\|b(\theta,\mu)-b(\vartheta,\nu)\|\le L\|\theta-\vartheta\|+LW_2(\mu,\nu).\] The Moreau specialization substitutes the already proved score constant \(L_1+\eta^{-1}\).




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Drift\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}wasserstein2}: Corrected W\ensuremath{{}_{2}} version of Lemma C.1 for an actual gradient-Lipschitz joint potential and actual finite-second-moment latent probability laws.
\item \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Drift\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}wasserstein2}: For split Moreau smoothing, the actual averaged drift has coefficient \texttt{L\ensuremath{{}_{1}}+1/\allowbreak{}eta}; the finite moments and all integral guards remain explicit.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Construct the mean-\allowbreak{}field L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} integral equations}{Construct the mean-field L² integral equations}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldProcess-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Process.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Work on a probability space \(Q\), with \(H=L^2(Q;\mathbb R^{d_\theta+d_x})\). A potential \(V\) with globally \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient defines the actual mean-field drift. Its continuous finite-horizon integral solution is constructed by the Banach-space Picard theorem.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Expectation is a bounded linear map from L\ensuremath{{}^{2}}}{Expectation is a bounded linear map from L²}}


The norm-of-integral inequality and \(\|X\|_{L^1}\le\|X\|_{L^2}\) on a probability space give \(\|\mathbb E_Q X\|\le\|X\|_2\). Bochner additivity and scalar linearity therefore define a bounded linear map \(\mathcal E:H\to E\) with operator norm at most one. The evaluation lemma records that its value is the actual integral.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}lp\_\allowbreak{}expectation\_\allowbreak{}le}: On a probability space, the actual expectation is bounded by the L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} norm.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Expectation\allowbreak{}CLM}: Actual Bochner expectation as a bounded linear map out of L\ensuremath{{}^{2}}.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Coordinate projections and a parameter inclusion}{Coordinate projections and a parameter inclusion}}


Define \(P_x(\theta,x)=x\) and \(I_\theta(\theta)=(\theta,0)\). Their coordinate identities follow by evaluating the definitions. The Euclidean square identity \(\|(\theta,0)\|^2=\|\theta\|^2\), followed by nonnegative square-root cancellation, proves that \(I_\theta\) is an isometry.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{joint\allowbreak{}Latent\allowbreak{}CLM}: The latent component of one parameter/\allowbreak{}latent joint vector.
\item \texttt{joint\allowbreak{}Parameter\allowbreak{}Inclusion\allowbreak{}CLM}: Embed only a parameter block into the joint space.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A true latent mask erases all parameter coordinates}{A true latent mask erases all parameter coordinates}}


Set \(M_x=I-I_\theta P_\theta\). Its parameter block is zero and its latent block is unchanged. Expanding the Euclidean squared norm shows \(\|M_xz\|^2\le\|z\|^2\), since the discarded parameter square is nonnegative. Thus the mask is nonexpansive and remains bounded after composition on \(L^2\).




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{joint\allowbreak{}Latent\allowbreak{}Mask\allowbreak{}CLM}: The actual latent-coordinate projection on the joint space.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Average exactly the parameter block}{Average exactly the parameter block}}


Construct the bounded linear map

\[\mathcal M X=\operatorname{const}(I_\theta P_\theta\mathbb E_QX)+M_xX.\]

The expectation estimate and coordinate contractions bound each term by \(\|X\|_2\), so \(\|\mathcal M X\|_2\le2\|X\|_2\). Linearity then proves \(\operatorname{Lip}(\mathcal M)\le2\). This is a conservative sufficient constant.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Average\allowbreak{}CLM}: Average the parameter coordinates and retain the latent coordinates as actual random variables. This is a bounded linear map, not an assumed drift.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Define the actual drift and apply Picard}{Define the actual drift and apply Picard}}


Set \(\mathcal A_V(X)=\mathcal M(\nabla V(X))\). The Nemytskii map for the actual Lipschitz gradient preserves \(L^2\) and has constant \(L\), so \(\operatorname{Lip}(\mathcal A_V)\le2L\). For any continuous \(H\)-valued input \(B\) with \(B_0=0\), and any \(T\ge0\), Picard constructs continuous \(Y\) with \(Y_0=Y^{\rm in}\) and

\[Y_t-Y^{\rm in}=-\int_0^t\mathcal A_V(Y_s)ds+B_t,\qquad0\le t\le T.\]

The proof is an existence theorem in the actual complete Banach space \(H\).




The averaging operator retains the latent gradient pointwise and replaces the parameter gradient by its expectation as a constant random variable. Expectation is a bounded linear map \(L^2\to H\) of norm at most one on a probability space, and the coordinate masks are orthogonal contractions. Thus the averaged drift is globally Lipschitz once the true joint score is Lipschitz. Picard's theorem applies in the complete Hilbert space of square-integrable joint states to a continuous additive input, constructing the actual integral solution on every finite horizon.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Drift}: The actual mean-field drift: expected parameter force and pointwise latent force.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Drift\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}: A conservative global Lipschitz constant for the constructed drift.
\item \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}lp\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\_\allowbreak{}integral}: Picard constructs the continuous mean-field L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} integral solution from its actual gradient and a continuous L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} additive input on every finite horizon.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Verify the intended block formulas in L\ensuremath{{}^{2}}}{Verify the intended block formulas in L²}}


Almost everywhere representative equalities show

\[(\mathcal M X)(\omega)=(\mathbb E_Q P_\theta X,\,P_xX(\omega)).\]

Applying parameter projection gives the constant random variable \(\operatorname{const}(P_\theta\mathbb E_QX)\); applying latent projection gives \(P_xX\). The proofs explicitly combine the almost everywhere addition, constant and composition identities, so they are equalities in \(L^2\).




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Average\allowbreak{}CLM\_\allowbreak{}coe}: The averaging map has exactly its intended pointwise block action.
\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Average\allowbreak{}CLM\_\allowbreak{}parameter}: Averaging makes the parameter block a constant random variable.
\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Average\allowbreak{}CLM\_\allowbreak{}latent}: Averaging leaves the actual latent random variable unchanged.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Identify the parameter force with the true marginal integral}{Identify the parameter force with the true marginal integral}}


Define \(a_V(X)=P_\theta\mathbb E_Q\nabla V(X)\), a continuous map on \(H\). The drift blocks are \(\operatorname{const}(a_V(X))\) and \(P_x\nabla V(X)\). If \(P_\theta X=\operatorname{const}(\theta)\), reconstruct \(X=(\theta,P_xX)\) almost everywhere. Commute the bounded projection through expectation and use the pushforward integral formula to prove

\[a_V(X)=\int\nabla_\theta V(\theta,x)\,\mathcal L_Q(P_xX)(dx).\]


\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Parameter\allowbreak{}Force}: The actual expected parameter force of a square-integrable joint vector.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Drift\_\allowbreak{}parameter}: The parameter projection of the actual drift is its expected force.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Drift\_\allowbreak{}latent}: The latent projection of the actual drift is pointwise latent gradient.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Parameter\allowbreak{}Force\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Drift}: The expected force equals the actual mean-field drift integral whenever the input parameter is deterministic. The latent marginal is its actual law.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Project the integral equation and construct a deterministic parameter}{Project the integral equation and construct a deterministic parameter}}


Suppose the initial parameter is constant \(\theta_0\) and \(P_\theta B_t=0\). Bounded linear maps commute with the Bochner time integral, so projecting the constructed equation gives \(P_\theta Y_t=\operatorname{const}(\theta_t)\), where \(\theta_t=\theta_0-\int_0^t a_V(Y_s)ds\). The continuous primitive proves continuity of \(\theta\). Set \(\mu_t=\mathcal L_Q(P_xY_t)\) and insert the force identity:

\[\theta_t-\theta_0=-\int_0^t b_V(\theta_s,\mu_s)ds,\qquad P_xY_t-P_xY_0=-\int_0^tP_x\nabla V(Y_s)ds+P_xB_t.\]

Both identities hold in their stated deterministic or \(L^2\) sense on the finite horizon.




Project the constructed joint equation onto the parameter and latent blocks. Continuous linear maps commute with the Bochner time integral. Since the additive input has zero parameter block, its parameter coordinate remains a constant random variable, represented by a deterministic continuous path \(\theta_t\). The force identity replaces expectation of the joint parameter score by its integral under the actual latent pushforward law \(\mu_t\), giving \[\theta_t-\theta_0=-\int_0^t\left[\int\nabla_\theta V(\theta_s,x)\,d\mu_s(x)\right]ds.\] The latent projection yields the corresponding drift-plus-noise equation. The parameter law is therefore derived from the constructed process, not introduced as a free auxiliary measure path.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}equation}: Projection of the constructed equation preserves a deterministic parameter and gives its genuine integral equation.
\item \texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\_\allowbreak{}latent\_\allowbreak{}equation}: The latent component satisfies the actual projected gradient equation.
\item \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}lp\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\_\allowbreak{}equations}: The constructed solution has a continuous deterministic parameter, its actual latent marginal, and both mean-field integral equations. No solution trajectory or law evolution is assumed in this existence statement.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Construct a continuous latent-\allowbreak{}only Brownian input}{Construct a continuous latent-only Brownian input}}


Apply the latent mask to the actual coordinate Brownian \(L^2\) noise. Brownian second moments prove its continuity in \(L^2\) and its zero value at time zero. Masking makes its parameter projection zero at every time. No separate continuity assumption on a stochastic error process is used.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Noise}: Actual Brownian-coordinate inputs, with only their latent coordinates retained, give a concrete continuous L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} input. Noise continuity and its zero initial value are conclusions of the Brownian laws.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Specialize to Brownian input and to the split envelope}{Specialize to Brownian input and to the split envelope}}


The constructed Brownian input satisfies all hypotheses of the general integral existence theorem, so substitution gives the Brownian-driven \(L^2\) solution. For \(V=g_1+g_2^\eta\), the actual proximal calculus supplies the Lipschitz gradient constant \(L_1+\eta^{-1}\), which is included as a derived witness in the conclusion. These results establish continuous \(L^2\) integral equations; they do not assert a pathwise strong-SDE or a Fokker–Planck PDE theorem.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}lp\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\_\allowbreak{}integral\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}brownian}: Genuine finite-horizon mean-field L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} existence with a Brownian input. All stochastic input continuity is derived from the coordinate Brownian laws; no pathwise strong-SDE, independence or adaptedness assertion is made.
\item \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}lp\_\allowbreak{}moreau\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\_\allowbreak{}integral}: Split Moreau smoothing supplies the actual globally Lipschitz gradient needed by the mean-field construction. Its coefficient is \texttt{L\ensuremath{{}_{1}}+1/\allowbreak{}eta}.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Exact iid variance and the empirical parameter force}{Exact iid variance and the empirical parameter force}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldVariance-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Variance.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


On a probability space, square-integrable Hilbert-valued variables have integrable inner products. On the actual finite product law, independent centered samples yield an exact \(1/N\) variance identity.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Cancel every off-\allowbreak{}diagonal covariance}{Cancel every off-diagonal covariance}}


Realize the supplied \(L^2\) functions as \(L^2\) elements; Cauchy–Schwarz gives integrable pairwise inner products. Expand

\[\mathbb E\left\|\sum_iX_i\right\|^2=\sum_{i,j}\mathbb E\langle X_i,X_j\rangle.\]

For \(i\ne j\), independence factors the expectation into \(\langle\mathbb E X_i,\mathbb E X_j\rangle=0\). The diagonal terms are \(\mathbb E\|X_i\|^2\). Finite sums and established integrability justify the expansion.




Expand the Hilbert square as \(\|\sum_iX_i\|^2=\sum_{i,j}\langle X_i,X_j\rangle\). Each mixed product is integrable by the \(L^2\) inner-product bound, which justifies moving the finite double sum through the integral. For \(i\ne j\), independence factors \(\mathbb E\langle X_i,X_j\rangle\) into \(\langle\mathbb EX_i,\mathbb EX_j\rangle=0\). For \(i=j\), it is \(\mathbb E\|X_i\|^2\). The local facts \texttt{hint}, \texttt{hcross} and \texttt{hexpand} establish these prerequisites and identities separately. Centering without independence would leave the off-diagonal covariances.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{integrable\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}mem\allowbreak{}Lp}: Every inner product of two L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} random variables is integrable.
\item \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}centered\_\allowbreak{}independent}: Centered independent Hilbert-valued samples have exactly additive second moments. Independence is used for each mixed term.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Use the actual finite product for an exact sample-\allowbreak{}mean variance}{Use the actual finite product for an exact sample-mean variance}}


For a square-integrable observable \(\phi\) and a probability law \(\mu\), the finite product measure \(\mu^{\otimes N}\) constructs the independent samples. The proof centers \(\phi\), expands the squared Hilbert norm, and uses independence and zero mean to cancel every off-diagonal inner product. The exact result is


\[
\mathbb E_{\mu^{\otimes N}}
 \left\|\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N\phi(X_i)-\mathbb E_\mu\phi\right\|^2
 =\frac1N\mathbb E_\mu\|\phi-\mathbb E_\mu\phi\|^2.
\]


Taking square roots proves the \(N^{-1/2}\) sampling factor. The actual parameter-gradient observable is square integrable under finite-P\ensuremath{{}_{2}} latent laws, so the formula applies to the mean-field force.



The source’s exchangeability premise alone does not cancel these covariances. A quantitative chaos estimate needs controlled initialization, such as the actual iid construction used here. This exact sampling identity is a proved ingredient; by itself it is not a propagation-of-chaos theorem.




On the explicit product probability \(P=\mu^{\otimes N}\), set \(C(a)=\varphi(a)-\mathbb E_\mu\varphi\) and \(X_i(\omega)=C(\omega_i)\). Measure-preserving coordinate evaluation proves that each \(X_i\) has the same finite second moment, zero mean and law, and the product construction proves their joint independence. The exact centered-mean identity is \[\frac1N\sum_i\varphi(\omega_i)-\mathbb E\varphi=\frac1N\sum_iX_i(\omega).\] Thus the previous variance identity gives \[\mathbb E_P\left\|\frac1N\sum_i\varphi(\omega_i)-\mathbb E\varphi\right\|^2=\frac{\mathbb E_\mu\|\varphi-\mathbb E\varphi\|^2}{N}.\] In the code, \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Xi}, \texttt{hind}, \texttt{hmean}, \texttt{hvar} and \texttt{hid} verify each of these assertions. Taking the nonnegative square root gives the RMS version.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{empirical\allowbreak{}Average}: The empirical mean on the explicitly specified finite sample space.
\item \texttt{iid\_\allowbreak{}empirical\_\allowbreak{}variance}: The actual iid finite product gives the exact centered empirical variance. All moments are Bochner integrals, including the mean used for centering.
\item \texttt{iid\_\allowbreak{}empirical\_\allowbreak{}rms}: The same exact identity written as an RMS sampling error.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Specialize the variance to a true gradient observable}{Specialize the variance to a true gradient observable}}


Fix \(\theta\) and let \(\phi(x)=\nabla_\theta U(\theta,x)\). A Lipschitz joint gradient makes this observable Lipschitz, hence square integrable under any finite-second-moment latent law \(\mu\). Its expectation is exactly \(b_U(\theta,\mu)\). Substitute into the iid formula to obtain

\[\mathbb E\left\|\frac1N\sum_i\nabla_\theta U(\theta,X_i)-b_U(\theta,\mu)\right\|^2=\frac1N\mathbb E\|\nabla_\theta U(\theta,X)-b_U(\theta,\mu)\|^2.\]


For \(\varphi(x)=\nabla_\theta U(\theta,x)\), the parameter projection is nonexpansive and the full score is \(L\)-Lipschitz, so \(\varphi\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz in the latent variable. A latent law with finite second moment therefore makes this observable square integrable. Its Bochner mean is exactly \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Drift}, so the general iid identity applies without assuming a separate variance bound for this particular gradient observable.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{iid\_\allowbreak{}parameter\allowbreak{}Gradient\_\allowbreak{}variance}: Exact iid empirical parameter-gradient variance for the actual joint potential. The copies are independent under the displayed finite product.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Construct the physical particle drift in a coordinate L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} space}{Construct the physical particle drift in a coordinate L² space}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticleDrift-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Particle\allowbreak{}Drift.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Let \(H_N=L^2(P;\Theta)\times\prod_{i<N}L^2(P;X)\) with norm \(\|z\|=\max(\|z_\theta\|_2,\max_i\|z_i\|_2)\). This Banach norm controls every tagged coordinate directly, with no exchangeability hypothesis.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Coordinate inclusions and nonexpansive projections}{Coordinate inclusions and nonexpansive projections}}


Define the coordinate product state and the latent inclusion \(I_x(x)=(0,x)\). The Euclidean square proves \(\|I_xx\|=\|x\|\), while the latent projection has norm at most one. For any bounded linear pointwise contraction \(C\), integrating \(\|C(X)\|^2\le\|X\|^2\) proves \(\|C\circ X\|_2\le\|X\|_2\).




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Particle\allowbreak{}State}: Coordinate L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} space, with the usual product/\allowbreak{}finite-function maximum norm.
\item \texttt{joint\allowbreak{}Latent\allowbreak{}Inclusion\allowbreak{}CLM}: Embed a latent vector into the joint space.
\item \texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}linear\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le}: A pointwise nonexpansive linear map remains nonexpansive on L\ensuremath{{}^{2}}.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Assemble one actual joint random pair}{Assemble one actual joint random pair}}


Set \(J(\Theta,X)=I_\theta\Theta+I_xX\). Its representative is the genuine pair \((\Theta(\omega),X(\omega))\) almost everywhere. Linearity of the inclusions and their norm bounds give

\[\|J(\Theta,X)-J(\Theta',X')\|_2\le\|\Theta-\Theta'\|_2+\|X-X'\|_2.\]


\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Joint\allowbreak{}Pair}: Pair the common parameter random variable with one latent variable.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Define the gradient and empirical particle drift}{Define the gradient and empirical particle drift}}


Apply the actual gradient \(\nabla V\) to each pair \(J(\Theta,X_i)\). The parameter force is the empirical average and each latent force is its own gradient block:

\[\mathcal A_N(z)=\left(\frac1N\sum_i\nabla_\theta V(\Theta,X_i),\ (\nabla_xV(\Theta,X_i))_i\right).\]

Almost everywhere composition and finite-sum identities verify these exact coordinate formulas. The definition does not replace the empirical parameter force by a mean-field expectation.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Particle\allowbreak{}Gradient}: The actual joint gradient at the common parameter and the selected particle.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Particle\allowbreak{}Drift}: The actual particle drift: averaged parameter force and individual latent forces.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Particle\allowbreak{}Gradient\_\allowbreak{}coe}: The gradient random variable has the actual parameter/\allowbreak{}particle coordinates.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Particle\allowbreak{}Drift\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}coe}: The displayed common-parameter force is precisely the empirical mean.
\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Particle\allowbreak{}Drift\_\allowbreak{}latent\_\allowbreak{}coe}: Each latent force is the latent block of its own actual joint gradient.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Dimension-\allowbreak{}free Lipschitz control constructs the actual solution}{Dimension-free Lipschitz control constructs the actual solution}}


Each coordinate difference is bounded by the product-state norm. The joint-pair estimate therefore gives \(\|\nabla V(J_i z)-\nabla V(J_i w)\|_2\le2L\|z-w\|\). Averaging \(N\) parameter terms and dividing by \(N>0\) preserves this bound; the maximum of the latent terms also preserves it. Hence \(\operatorname{Lip}(\mathcal A_N)\le2L\), independently of \(N\). Picard constructs continuous \(Z\) satisfying \(Z_t-Z_0=-\int_0^t\mathcal A_N(Z_s)ds+B_t\) for any continuous input with \(B_0=0\).




The particle state is the product of one parameter \(L^2\) state and a finite family of latent \(L^2\) states, with the product maximum norm. Reconstructing pair \(i\) gives \(\|\operatorname{pair}(\theta,x_i)-\operatorname{pair}(\vartheta,y_i)\|_2\le\|\theta-\vartheta\|_2+\|x_i-y_i\|_2\le2\|z-w\|\). Applying the \(L\)-Lipschitz score gives \(2L\|z-w\|\) for each pair force. Nonexpansive projections preserve this bound; averaging parameter forces cancels the factor \(N\), and taking a maximum over latent coordinates introduces none. This proves the dimension-independent \(2L\) constant used by Picard to construct the particle integral equation.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Particle\allowbreak{}Drift\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}: A global dimension-free Lipschitz bound in the coordinate-maximum L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} norm.
\item \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}lp\_\allowbreak{}particle\_\allowbreak{}integral}: Picard constructs the actual finite-particle coordinate integral equations from continuous additive inputs, without a stochastic convergence premise.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Actual iid L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} copies and their exact drift residual}{Actual iid L² copies and their exact drift residual}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldCopies-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Copies.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Starting from one mean-field random pair on \(Q\), construct copies on the actual finite product \(P=Q^{\otimes N}\). When its parameter is deterministic, all copies share that parameter and their latent coordinates are iid.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Measure-\allowbreak{}preserving coordinate pullback is a bounded linear copy map}{Measure-preserving coordinate pullback is a bounded linear copy map}}


For product coordinate \(\omega_i\), define \(C_iX=X\circ\omega_i\). Evaluation is measure preserving, so pullback is an \(L^2\) isometry and a bounded linear map. Its representative equals \(X(\omega_i)\) almost everywhere. Coordinate substitution proves \(C_i(CX)=C(C_iX)\) for bounded linear \(C\), and \(C_i(\operatorname{const}x)=\operatorname{const}x\).




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{iid\allowbreak{}Copy\allowbreak{}CLM}: A genuine iid copy of a random variable on the finite product space.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Center and compute the true empirical fluctuation norm}{Center and compute the true empirical fluctuation norm}}


For \(N>0\), define \(\mathcal C X=X-\operatorname{const}(\mathbb EX)\) and \(\Delta_N(X)=N^{-1}\sum_iC_iX-\operatorname{const}(\mathbb EX)\). Almost everywhere finite-sum identities identify its representative with the genuine sample mean. The exact iid variance formula gives

\[\|\Delta_N(X)\|_{L^2(P)}^2=\frac1N\|\mathcal C X\|_{L^2(Q)}^2,\qquad\|\Delta_N(X)\|_2=\frac{\|\mathcal C X\|_2}{\sqrt N}.\]


The coordinate pullback \(X\mapsto X(\omega_i)\) is a linear isometry because coordinate evaluation preserves \(Q\). It therefore commutes with continuous linear coordinate projections and retains constants. Center the original variable by its actual Bochner expectation, then form the empirical fluctuation of these genuine iid copies. The representative theorem identifies this \(L^2\) object with the usual centered sample average. Substituting the exact product variance identity gives \[\|\text{empirical fluctuation}\|_2^2=\frac{\|X-\mathbb EX\|_2^2}{N},\] and nonnegative square roots give its precise \(N^{-1/2}\) norm.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Center\allowbreak{}CLM}: Center an actual square-integrable random variable by its expectation.
\item \texttt{iid\allowbreak{}Empirical\allowbreak{}Fluctuation}: The actual finite-product centered empirical mean as an L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} random variable.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Build the reference particle state without altering its parameter}{Build the reference particle state without altering its parameter}}


Bounded linear maps commute with actual Bochner expectation, and constants have their stated expectation on a probability space. Define

\[\mathcal R_N(X)=\left(\operatorname{const}(\mathbb E P_\theta X),\ (P_xC_iX)_i\right).\]

If \(P_\theta X=\operatorname{const}\theta\), the parameter block of \(\mathcal R_N(X)\) is exactly \(\operatorname{const}\theta\). The coordinate lemmas are direct evaluations of this bounded linear construction.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Expectation\allowbreak{}CLM\_\allowbreak{}comp\allowbreak{}Lp}: Actual expectation commutes with any bounded linear map.
\item \texttt{iid\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Copies\allowbreak{}CLM}: Mean-field parameter expectation and genuine iid latent copies.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Reconstruct each reference pair and commute the true drift}{Reconstruct each reference pair and commute the true drift}}


Reassembling a variable from its parameter and latent projections returns the same \(L^2\) element. With deterministic parameter this proves \(J_i\mathcal R_N(X)=C_iX\). A Lipschitz pointwise drift also commutes with coordinate pullback, since both representatives are the same function \(b(X(\omega_i))\) almost everywhere.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Joint\allowbreak{}Pair\_\allowbreak{}projections}: Reconstructing one pair from a joint random variable loses no coordinates.
\item \texttt{iid\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Copies\_\allowbreak{}joint}: Each reference pair is the selected genuine joint copy when its parameter is deterministic. This is an equality of actual L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} random variables.
\item \texttt{iid\allowbreak{}Copy\allowbreak{}CLM\_\allowbreak{}lp\allowbreak{}Drift}: Actual pointwise Lipschitz drift commutes with an iid pullback.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Only the empirical parameter block remains in the residual}{Only the empirical parameter block remains in the residual}}


Apply the particle drift to \(\mathcal R_N(X)\), and compare with \(\mathcal R_N(\mathcal A_V(X))\). Each latent block agrees exactly by the preceding commutation. The parameter difference is the centered empirical gradient:

\[\mathcal A_N(\mathcal R_NX)-\mathcal R_N(\mathcal A_VX)=\left(\Delta_N(P_\theta\nabla V(X)),0\right).\]

The product maximum norm and exact fluctuation identity therefore give residual norm \(\|\mathcal C(P_\theta\nabla V(X))\|_2/\sqrt N\). This equality is the source of the particle comparison rate.




The reference parameter is deterministic, so recombining it with latent copy \(i\) recovers exactly joint copy \(i\). The true score commutes with coordinate pullback, which identifies each reference pair force with its iid copied score. In the latent blocks the particle drift and copied mean-field drift cancel exactly. In the parameter block their difference is \[\frac1N\sum_i\nabla_\theta V(\theta,X_i)-\mathbb E\nabla_\theta V(\theta,X).\] The final norm theorem invokes the exact empirical-fluctuation identity, leaving the centered single-copy force norm divided by \(\sqrt N\). This establishes the drift defect from the actual product construction.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{iid\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Copies\_\allowbreak{}drift\_\allowbreak{}residual}: The finite-particle drift defect at the constructed iid reference is exactly its centered empirical parameter-gradient error. The latent defect is zero.
\item \texttt{iid\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Copies\_\allowbreak{}drift\_\allowbreak{}residual\_\allowbreak{}norm}: The actual drift defect has its exact independent-sampling norm.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Grönwall comparison with an extra continuous noise input}{Grönwall comparison with an extra continuous noise input}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldForcing-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Forcing.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


In a Banach space let \(F\) be \(L\)-Lipschitz. Compare actual integral equations with a common input \(B\), one extra continuous input \(A\), and a reference forcing \(G\) satisfying a finite drift defect.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Subtract the extra input before applying integral Grönwall}{Subtract the extra input before applying integral Grönwall}}


Suppose \(\|A_t\|\le a\), \(\|F(Y_t)-G_t\|\le C\), and

\[X_t-x=-\int_0^tF(X_s)ds+B_t+A_t,\qquad Y_t-x=-\int_0^tG_sds+B_t.\]

Set \(D_t=X_t-Y_t-A_t\). The common input cancels and \(\|D_t\|\le\int_0^t[L\|D_s\|+La+C]ds\). Grönwall yields \(\|X_t-Y_t\|\le(C+La)\Psi(L,T)+a\) on \([0,T]\). The proof uses continuous integral equations and never differentiates the additional input.




The inputs \(B,A\) may be continuous Brownian paths in \(L^2\); differentiating them would be invalid. Instead set \(D_t=X_t-Y_t-A_t\). Subtracting the two actual integral equations cancels \(B\) and \(A\), leaving \[D_t=-\int_0^t[F(X_s)-G_s]ds,\quad D_0=0.\] Continuity of the integrands proves that this remainder has derivative \(-F(X_t)+G_t\).



If \(F\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz, \(\|A_t\|\le a\) and \(\|F(Y_t)-G_t\|\le C\), then \[\|D'_t\|\le L\|D_t\|+C+La.\] The right-derivative Grönwall theorem gives \(\|D_t\|\le\operatorname{gronwallBound}(0,L,C+La,T)\) uniformly on \([0,T]\). Adding back \(A_t\) produces the final \(+a\). In Lean, \texttt{h\allowbreak{}DI} identifies the differentiable integral remainder, \texttt{hderiv} proves its derivative, and \texttt{hb} establishes this exact scalar forcing.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}forcing\_\allowbreak{}perturbation}: Actual integral equations with common input, an additional continuous input, and a uniformly controlled reference-force defect satisfy this finite-horizon bound. Neither a derivative of the input nor an error recurrence is assumed.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Factor a common inverse sampling scale}{Factor a common inverse sampling scale}}


The explicit zero-initial-error Grönwall function is linear in its forcing coefficient, including its \(L=0\) branch. Thus \(\operatorname{GB}(0,L,C/r,T)=\operatorname{GB}(0,L,C,T)/r\). If \(r>0\), \(\|A_t\|\le a/r\) and \(\|F(Y_t)-G_t\|\le C/r\), substitute into the preceding theorem and factor:

\[\|X_t-Y_t\|\le\frac{(C+La)\Psi(L,T)+a}{r}.\]


The zero-initial-error Grönwall response is linear in its forcing: \(G(0,L,C/r,T)=G(0,L,C,T)/r\), also in the \(L=0\) branch. Apply the preceding theorem with forcing \(C/r\) and extra-input bound \(a/r\), where \(r>0\). Factor \((C+La)/r\) and use that homogeneity to obtain the common \(1/r\) factor in the conclusion. In the particle application \(r=\sqrt N\), so the entire coefficient is independent of \(N\).




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{gronwall\allowbreak{}Bound\_\allowbreak{}zero\_\allowbreak{}div}: The zero-initial-error Grönwall response is homogeneous in its forcing.
\item \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}forcing\_\allowbreak{}perturbation\_\allowbreak{}scaled}: The uniform forcing and input bounds of order \texttt{1/\allowbreak{}r} give the same scale for the actual solution error, with an explicit coefficient independent of \texttt{r}.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Construct the empirical parameter Brownian input}{Construct the empirical parameter Brownian input}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldNoise-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Noise.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


On \(Q^{\otimes N}\), \(N>0\), use copy zero of a supplied \(d_\theta\)-coordinate Brownian source for the common parameter input. The actual noise amplitude is \(\sqrt{2/N}\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Pull back Brownian coordinates and define their scaled L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} noise}{Pull back Brownian coordinates and define their scaled L² noise}}


Coordinate evaluation on the finite product is measure preserving, so each pulled-back scalar process retains its standard Brownian law. Define \(A_t=N^{-1/2}\sqrt2(B_t^\theta-B_0^\theta)\) as an actual \(L^2\) element. The Brownian-noise construction proves continuity in \(L^2\) and value zero at time zero; scalar multiplication preserves both facts.




Choose copy zero on the finite iid product, which exists because \(N>0\). Coordinate evaluation is measure preserving, so pulling back each supplied standard Brownian coordinate preserves its Brownian law. Multiply its Langevin noise \(\sqrt2W_t\) by \(N^{-1/2}\), producing the actual common parameter input. The \(L^2\)-valued Brownian construction proves continuity in time and zero initial value; neither property is assumed for a newly introduced noise process.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{empirical\allowbreak{}Parameter\_\allowbreak{}pre\allowbreak{}Brownian}: Each coordinate of the common parameter source has the actual Brownian law after pullback to the finite iid product.
\item \texttt{empirical\allowbreak{}Parameter\allowbreak{}Noise}: The actual parameter Brownian input, with the particle scaling.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Derive the exact norm and its N-\allowbreak{}independent horizon coefficient}{Derive the exact norm and its N-independent horizon coefficient}}


The coordinate second moments sum to \(d_\theta t\). Consequently

\[\|A_t\|_2=\frac{\sqrt{2d_\theta}\sqrt t}{\sqrt N}\le\frac{\sqrt{2d_\theta}\sqrt T}{\sqrt N},\qquad0\le t\le T.\]

The theorem follows by the exact Brownian norm identity and monotonicity of the square root. The canonical specialization uses the already constructed Gaussian time-indicator Brownian source, so it supplies a concrete instance rather than postulating one.




Each parameter Brownian coordinate has variance \(t\), so the vector Langevin noise has squared \(L^2\) norm \(2d_\theta t\). Scalar multiplication by \(N^{-1/2}\) gives the exact identity \[\|A_N(t)\|_2=\frac{\sqrt{2d_\theta}\sqrt t}{\sqrt N}.\] Monotonicity of the square root gives the uniform horizon bound with \(\sqrt T\). The canonical corollary merely substitutes the already constructed Gaussian time-indicator Brownian source, thereby discharging existence of a source with these moments.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{empirical\allowbreak{}Parameter\allowbreak{}Noise\_\allowbreak{}norm}: The exact Brownian second-moment scale of the actual parameter input.
\item \texttt{empirical\allowbreak{}Parameter\allowbreak{}Noise\_\allowbreak{}bound}: A finite-horizon bound with an explicit coefficient independent of \texttt{N}.
\item \texttt{canonical\_\allowbreak{}empirical\allowbreak{}Parameter\allowbreak{}Noise\_\allowbreak{}bound}: A concrete source exists: the Brownian coordinates are the already constructed independent Gaussian time-indicator processes.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Constructed iid particle comparison with uniform timewise L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} control}{Constructed iid particle comparison with uniform timewise L² control}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticles-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Particles.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


For a potential with globally \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient, compare a constructed mean-field solution with actual finite-particle coordinate solutions on the iid product source. The coefficient is chosen before all particle counts and the norm is the supremum of timewise \(L^2\) errors.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The actual centered force is continuous and bounded on a finite horizon}{The actual centered force is continuous and bounded on a finite horizon}}


Define \(\mathcal C_V(X)=P_\theta\nabla V(X)-\operatorname{const}\mathbb E[P_\theta\nabla V(X)]\) using bounded linear centering and the actual Lipschitz drift. This composition is continuous. For continuous \(Y_t\), the image of compact \([0,T]\) is compact; its centered-force norm therefore has a finite bound \(C\ge0\). This \(C\) depends on the reference and horizon and is chosen independently of \(N\).




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{centered\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Force}: The centered actual parameter force of one mean-field copy.
\item \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}centered\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Force\_\allowbreak{}bound}: Continuity of the constructed reference supplies a finite force coefficient on a compact horizon. This coefficient is independent of particle count.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Copies inherit the actual equation by bounded linearity}{Copies inherit the actual equation by bounded linearity}}


The copy map \(\mathcal R_N\) is bounded linear, so it commutes with the Bochner time integral and preserves differences. Apply it to the mean-field equation to get the exact copied-reference forcing. Define

\[C_T=2\big[(C+2Lb)\Psi(2L,T)+b\big].\]

Here \(C\) bounds the actual centered force and \(b/\sqrt N\) bounds the extra parameter input. The factor two converts the coordinate maximum norm into the sum of parameter and one latent error.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{iid\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Copies\_\allowbreak{}integral\_\allowbreak{}equation}: The actual iid reference inherits its integral equation by a bounded linear map, so its forcing is constructed from the mean-field solution.
\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Particle\allowbreak{}Constant}: Finite-horizon coefficient in the tagged-coordinate L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} comparison. \texttt{C} bounds the actual centered reference force, and \texttt{b} bounds the parameter Brownian input after multiplication by \texttt{sqrt N}.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Construct the particle solution and use the exact iid residual}{Construct the particle solution and use the exact iid residual}}


For each \(N>0\), Picard constructs the actual particle solution \(Z\) with copied latent input and any continuous extra parameter input \(A\) bounded by \(b/\sqrt N\). The copied mean-field reference has drift residual norm exactly \(\|\mathcal C_V(Y_t)\|_2/\sqrt N\le C/\sqrt N\). Apply the forcing-comparison theorem to the \(2L\)-Lipschitz particle drift. Both tagged coordinate errors are at most the product-state norm, hence

\[\|\Theta_t^N-\theta_t\|_2+\|X_t^{i,N}-X_t^i\|_2\le C_T/\sqrt N,\qquad0\le t\le T.\]

The theorem retains the constructed integral equation and exact iid initial state. It does not assume the final rate.




Continuity of the single-copy centered force along the constructed mean-field path implies a finite bound \(C\) on the compact interval \([0,T]\). Construct the actual particle process by Picard with copied latent input and the extra parameter input \(A_N\). The copied reference equation follows by commuting its bounded linear copy map through the time integral. Its drift defect is at most \(C/\sqrt N\), by the exact iid residual theorem, and \(\|A_N\|\le b/\sqrt N\).



The scaled integral-forcing theorem, with particle Lipschitz constant \(2L\), yields \[\|Z_t-R_t\|\le\frac{\operatorname{gronwallBound}(0,2L,C+2Lb,T)+b}{\sqrt N}.\] Each parameter or tagged-latent difference is bounded by the maximum product norm; their sum is therefore at most twice this expression. This accounts for the factor two in \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Particle\allowbreak{}Constant}. The reference and \(C\) are chosen before \(N\), making the coefficient independent of particle count.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}particle\_\allowbreak{}comparison\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\_\allowbreak{}solution}: The constructed particle solution is uniformly close, in each coordinate L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} norm, to the genuine iid copies of an actual mean-field integral solution. The reference force coefficient is finite and independent of \texttt{N}.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Construct both systems with actual Brownian inputs}{Construct both systems with actual Brownian inputs}}


The actual centered reference force is a continuous L\ensuremath{{}^{2}}-valued function of time. Compactness of \([0,T]\) supplies a finite \(C\ge0\) such that


\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}\|
 \nabla_\theta V(Y_t)-\mathbb E_Q\nabla_\theta V(Y_t)\|_2\le C.
\]


This \(C\) is chosen \textbf{before the quantifier over all particle counts}; it depends on the reference potential, initial law and horizon, not on \(N\). Subtract the common latent input and the extra parameter Brownian input in the actual integral equations. The proof applies Grönwall to the remaining drift integral, so it never differentiates Brownian noise. Define


\[
\Psi(a,T)=\begin{cases}(e^{aT}-1)/a,&a>0,\\T,&a=0,\end{cases}
\qquad
C_T=2\big[(C+2Lb)\Psi(2L,T)+b\big].
\]


For every \(N>0\), the theorem constructs the actual particle integral solution with the prescribed iid initialization and proves, for every tagged particle \(i\),


\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}\left(
 \|\Theta_t^N-\theta_t\|_2+\|X_t^{i,N}-X_t^i\|_2\right)
 \le\frac{C_T}{\sqrt N}.
\]


For the actual split-smoothed potential \(V=g_1+g_2^\eta\), use the derived constant \(L=L_1+1/\eta\). The coefficient can depend on \(\eta\); uniformity in smoothing is not claimed. This is a constructed iid propagation-of-chaos replacement in \textbf{uniform timewise L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} norm}. It does not move the time supremum inside the expectation, as in printed Proposition C.2; the expected path-supremum norm is stronger; the proved uniform timewise L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} estimate does not establish it.

\item \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}brownian\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\_\allowbreak{}particle\_\allowbreak{}comparison}: Both the mean-field reference and finite-particle coordinate processes are constructed from actual Brownian-coordinate inputs. The parameter-noise \texttt{N-\allowbreak{}\ensuremath{{}^{1}}/\allowbreak{}\ensuremath{{}^{2}}} bound is proved from those laws by \texttt{empirical\allowbreak{}Parameter\allowbreak{}Noise\_\allowbreak{}bound}. The conclusion controls each time's L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} norm uniformly over the horizon.

\section{\texorpdfstring{An independent Brownian family for all actual particle coordinates}{An independent Brownian family for all actual particle coordinates}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldBrownianFamily-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Family.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


On \(Q^{\otimes N}\), choose parameter Brownian coordinates from copy zero and latent coordinate \((i,j)\) from copy \(i\). Injective selection proves independence of complete selected coordinate paths.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Select distinct copy/\allowbreak{}coordinate pairs}{Select distinct copy/coordinate pairs}}


Map a parameter index \(j\) to \((0,\mathrm{parameter}\ j)\), and a latent index \((i,j)\) to \((i,\mathrm{latent}\ j)\). The parameter/\allowbreak{}latent tags make the two kinds disjoint, and equality within either kind forces equality of the original indices. This proves injectivity. Define the family by evaluating the corresponding source Brownian coordinate in the selected copy; the displayed coordinate identities unfold the selection.




The index map sends a parameter coordinate \(j\) to \((0,\theta_j)\) and a latent particle coordinate \((i,j)\) to \((i,x_j)\). These are distinct even for the first particle because the parameter and latent coordinates use different summands of the joint index. The injectivity proof checks the four parameter/\allowbreak{}latent cases explicitly. The two evaluation lemmas then identify the selected paths with the intended common parameter noise and each particle's own latent noise.

\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Index}: Select a distinct base-copy/\allowbreak{}coordinate pair for every particle-noise coordinate.
\item \texttt{particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Index\_\allowbreak{}injective}: The common parameter uses copy zero; latent blocks use their respective copies.
\item \texttt{particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Family}: The actual joint Brownian source for the interacting particle coordinates.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Each selected process retains its Brownian law}{Each selected process retains its Brownian law}}


Pullback along product evaluation is measure preserving, so every selected scalar process is standard pre-Brownian. If all source evaluations are measurable, composition with the selected coordinate map proves every new evaluation measurable as well.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Family\_\allowbreak{}pre\allowbreak{}Brownian}: Every selected coordinate is an actual standard pre-Brownian motion.
\item \texttt{particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Family\_\allowbreak{}measurable}: Measurable source evaluations remain measurable after selecting a copy.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Flatten independent rows and select an injective subfamily}{Flatten independent rows and select an injective subfamily}}


The finite product makes the complete Brownian rows independent. Inside each row, the supplied full coordinate paths are independent. The product-family independence theorem flattens these into independent variables indexed by \((i,j)\). Precomposing with the injective selection proves independence of all particle coordinates, including parameter and first-particle latent blocks. The canonical Brownian source supplies both the row laws and full-path independence, yielding an explicit complete family.




First pull each whole Brownian row from the source to one coordinate of the finite product; distinct rows are independent by the product measure. Within a row the supplied coordinate paths are independent, and measure-preserving evaluation retains their joint law. The helper \texttt{i\allowbreak{}Indep\allowbreak{}Fun\_\allowbreak{}uncurry'} flattens these two levels into a jointly independent family indexed by all copy/\allowbreak{}coordinate pairs. Finally, precomposition with the injective index map selects the intended subfamily and preserves independence. This proves independence of full coordinate paths, including the parameter path and the first particle's latent path; it does not assert independence of interacting particle states.

\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Family\_\allowbreak{}paths\_\allowbreak{}independent}: Full coordinate-path independence is derived on the actual finite product. There is no assumption of independent particles after interaction.
\item \texttt{canonical\_\allowbreak{}particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Family}: The concrete canonical Gaussian time-indicator construction supplies all selected scalar Brownian laws and their full-path independence.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Concrete independent initialization and Brownian paths}{Concrete independent initialization and Brownian paths}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldInitialSource-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Initial\allowbreak{}Source.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Given a latent probability law \(\mu\) with finite second moment and a deterministic \(\theta_0\), use the product \(Q=\mu\otimes Q_B\), where \(Q_B\) is the canonical joint Brownian source. This constructs all initialization and source hypotheses from primitive data.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Product source and its measurable Brownian evaluations}{Product source and its measurable Brownian evaluations}}


The source is the actual product law \(\mu\otimes Q_B\). Brownian coordinates use only its second factor, so measure-preserving second projection transfers their standard Brownian laws. Coordinate measurability assembles into full-path measurability. The joint path pushforward remains exactly its canonical product law, proving independence of the complete coordinate paths after initialization is added.



The named probability instance follows from the product measure identity \(Q(\Omega)=\mu(X)Q_B(\Omega_B)=1\cdot1=1\). This records normalization of the constructed source before any law or expectation is used.

\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Initial\allowbreak{}Source}: The supplied latent initialization law times the explicit Gaussian Brownian source.
\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Initial\allowbreak{}Brownian}: Brownian coordinates use the independent second factor of the source.
\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Initial\allowbreak{}Brownian\_\allowbreak{}pre\allowbreak{}Brownian}: Each coordinate has the actual standard Brownian law on the product source.
\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Initial\allowbreak{}Brownian\_\allowbreak{}paths\_\allowbreak{}independent}: The entire coordinate paths remain independent after adding initialization.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Build the square-\allowbreak{}integrable pair initialization}{Build the square-integrable pair initialization}}


The map \(x\mapsto(\theta_0,x)\) is nonexpansive because its parameter difference vanishes. The latent first-factor variable has finite second moment under the product law. Lipschitz composition therefore proves that \((\theta_0,X_0)\) is square integrable, and its actual \(L^2\) representative defines the initialization.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Initial\allowbreak{}Pair\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}: Holding the parameter fixed embeds latent vectors isometrically into pairs.
\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Initial\_\allowbreak{}mem\allowbreak{}Lp}: A fixed parameter and the actual latent initialization form an L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} joint vector.
\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Initial\allowbreak{}Lp}: The actual square-integrable initialization used by the integral constructors.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Retain the exact parameter, latent law and source independence}{Retain the exact parameter, latent law and source independence}}


The representative equals \((\theta_0,\omega_1)\) almost everywhere, so its parameter projection is \(\operatorname{const}\theta_0\), and its latent pushforward is exactly \(\mu\). The whole initialization depends only on the first factor while all Brownian paths depend only on the second. Product independence, followed by the almost everywhere representative substitution, proves independence of initialization and the entire Brownian family.




The source is the product of the supplied latent initial law and the canonical joint Brownian source. The initialized pair is \((\theta_0,x)\), a Lipschitz function of the first coordinate; its square integrability follows from the given latent second moment. The noise paths depend only on the second coordinate. Product independence therefore proves initialization/\allowbreak{}noise-path independence, while the projection identities prove that the parameter is the constant \(\theta_0\) and the latent law is exactly the supplied \(\mu\). This construction discharges the probabilistic inputs without asking for a pre-existing process on the user's probability space.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Initial\allowbreak{}Lp\_\allowbreak{}parameter}: The parameter block is exactly the constant initial parameter in L\ensuremath{{}^{2}}.
\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Initial\allowbreak{}Lp\_\allowbreak{}latent\_\allowbreak{}law}: Its latent marginal is exactly the supplied finite-second-moment law.
\item \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Initial\allowbreak{}Lp\_\allowbreak{}independent\_\allowbreak{}paths}: Initialization is independent of every Brownian coordinate at every time.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Discharge every scalar Brownian law and full-\allowbreak{}path independence}{Discharge every scalar Brownian law and full-path independence}}


The initialized source already has the true Brownian coordinate laws and independent full paths. The injective particle-coordinate selection on its finite product preserves both properties. Thus the parameter coordinates and every particle’s latent coordinates form one independent standard Brownian family, including the first-copy overlap resolved by distinct coordinate tags.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Construct the reference once, then every particle process}{Construct the reference once, then every particle process}}


Use the actual initial pair \((\theta_0,X_0)\) on \(\mu\otimes Q_B\), whose square integrability and deterministic parameter projection were proved. Substitute this source and the actual Brownian coordinate process into the Brownian comparison theorem. It constructs continuous \(Y,\theta\), a finite \(C\ge0\), and for every \(N>0\) a continuous actual particle solution \(Z^N\) with exact iid initialization. The equations retain latent noise \(\sqrt2 B^{x,i}\) and parameter noise \(\sqrt{2/N}B^\theta\), and prove

\[\sup_{0\le t\le T}\big(\|\Theta_t^N-\theta_t\|_2+\|X_t^{i,N}-X_t^i\|_2\big)\le\frac{C_T}{\sqrt N}.\]

The coefficient is selected before the quantifier over \(N\). This controls the supremum of \(L^2\) norms, without claiming an expected path supremum.




The primitive inputs are a latent probability law with finite second moment, a deterministic parameter, a globally Lipschitz joint score and a finite horizon. The initialized product source supplies all Brownian coordinate laws, their whole-path independence, and initialization/\allowbreak{}noise independence. Substitute these into the already proved Brownian comparison theorem to construct one mean-field reference, then a particle solution for every positive \(N\). The output retains both integral equations and the deterministic parameter equation alongside the bound. Its uniformity is \(\sup_{t\le T}\|Z_t-R_t\|_{L^2}\); no exchange of supremum and expectation is made.

\begin{itemize}

The displayed constant is independent of that count; its existence follows from proved finite-horizon moments of the reference.

Finiteness is established before converting the extended transport value to a real number.

W_2\!\left(\mathcal L(\Theta_t^N,X_t^{i,N}),\mathcal L(\theta_t,X_t)\right)

\section{\texorpdfstring{Strong convexity after full Moreau smoothing}{Strong convexity after full Moreau smoothing}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-StrongMoreau-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}Strong\allowbreak{}Moreau.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Smoothing an entire \(m\)-strongly convex potential changes its modulus to \(m_\lambda=m/(1+m\lambda)\). This proof needs \(m\ge0\), \(\lambda>0\), convex minimizer existence and a lower bound, without differentiability of the original function.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Two exact quadratic identities}{Two exact quadratic identities}}


For \(a+b=1\), expand inner products to prove \(\|au+bv\|^2=a\|u\|^2+b\|v\|^2-ab\|u-v\|^2\). The needed effective modulus follows from the exact square

\[m\|u\|^2+\frac{\|u-v\|^2}{\lambda}-\frac{m\|v\|^2}{1+m\lambda}=\frac{\|(1+m\lambda)u-v\|^2}{\lambda(1+m\lambda)}\ge0.\]


\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}convex\allowbreak{}Combo\_\allowbreak{}sq}: Exact square identity for a convex combination in a real inner product space.
\item \texttt{weighted\_\allowbreak{}quadratic\_\allowbreak{}lower}: The quadratic infimal convolution has the sharp effective modulus.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Compare actual minimizers}{Compare actual minimizers}}


If \(U\) is \(m\)-strongly convex, its actual full Moreau envelope is


\[
m_\lambda=\frac{m}{1+m\lambda}\text{-strongly convex}.
\]


The proof evaluates the envelope at the convex combination of its two actual proximal minimizers, applies strong convexity to \(U\), and uses the exact square identity for that combination. The remaining comparison is the weighted quadratic inequality


\[
\frac{m}{1+m\lambda}\|v\|^2
\le m\|u\|^2+\frac{\|u-v\|^2}{\lambda}.
\]


Completing the square proves this inequality with the displayed sharp modulus. No differentiability of \(U\) is needed. In contrast, smoothing only \(g_2\) preserves the modulus of the unsmoothed \(g_1\); the two constructions must not share a convexity constant by notation alone.




Let \(p=P_\lambda(x)\), \(q=P_\lambda(y)\), \(z=ax+by\), and test the objective at \(ap+bq\), with \(a+b=1\). The potential contributes the deficit \(\frac{mab}{2}\|p-q\|^2\); the quadratic penalty contributes \(\frac{ab}{2\lambda}\|(p-q)-(x-y)\|^2\). The weighted quadratic inequality bounds their sum below by \(\frac{ab}{2}\frac{m}{1+m\lambda}\|x-y\|^2\), proving the exact strong-convexity definition for the envelope. Positivity of \(\lambda(1+m\lambda)\) makes the completed-square remainder nonnegative.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}strong\allowbreak{}Convex}: The actual Moreau envelope preserves strong convexity with the exact reduced modulus.
\end{itemize}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Full-\allowbreak{}potential PIPULA and its smoothed-\allowbreak{}center theorem}{Full-potential PIPULA and its smoothed-center theorem}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-PIPULA-lean}
{\small\color{gray}Source module: \texttt{Proximal\allowbreak{}Langevin/\allowbreak{}PIPULA.\allowbreak{}lean}.\par}


Assume the full nonsmooth potential \(U\) is \(m\)-strongly convex and bounded below, with \(m,\gamma>0\). Global Lipschitzness of \(U\) is not required. The theorem concerns the maximizer of the marginal of \(U^\gamma\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The actual full-\allowbreak{}envelope modulus makes the step admissible}{The actual full-envelope modulus makes the step admissible}}


Set \(m_\gamma=m/(1+m\gamma)>0\). Since \(\gamma m_\gamma=m\gamma/(1+m\gamma)\le1\), we have \(\gamma(m_\gamma+\gamma^{-1})\le2\), equivalently \(\gamma\le2/(m_\gamma+\gamma^{-1})\). All multiplications and divisions have positive denominators.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{full\allowbreak{}Smoothing\allowbreak{}Modulus}: Strong convexity after smoothing the entire potential.
\item \texttt{pipula\_\allowbreak{}admissible\_\allowbreak{}step}: With smoothing equal to the time step, the envelope always meets the proved Gaussian ULA step restriction.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The proximal/\allowbreak{}noise law is the actual IPLA law}{The proximal/noise law is the actual IPLA law}}


The law is the pushforward of the exact PIPULA recursion. Set \(g_1=0\) and \(\eta=\gamma\) in the pointwise MYIPLA identity. Actual proximal optimality gives \(P_\gamma=I-\gamma\nabla U^\gamma\), so the full chain equals IPLA for \(U^\gamma\), and hence their pushforwards agree.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipula\allowbreak{}Laws}: The probability law defined by the paper's pairwise PIPULA recursion.
\item \texttt{pipula\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}ipla\allowbreak{}Laws}: Exact equality between the displayed PIPULA law and IPLA for the genuine full Moreau envelope.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Sharp concentration and Gaussian discretization}{Sharp concentration and Gaussian discretization}}


The algebraic reduction \(g_1=0\), \(\lambda=\gamma\) is proved for the actual update and its full recursion. Together with the envelope theory, it supplies genuine proximity, smoothness, and strong-convexity facts for a full-potential scheme.



It does not reproduce v3 Corollary A.6's original-center finite-time bound. In assumption B1, the displayed proximal inner-product deficit \(\mu\) gives the envelope-gradient constant \(\mu/\gamma\), since the residual is divided by \(\gamma\). The code records that exact implication. A scale-uniform PIPULA discretization theorem and an original-center estimate with the paper's rate remain unproved here; substituting a \(1/\gamma\) smoothness bound into a generic ULA estimate alone would not establish the claimed vanishing error.



A separate proved finite-time result is supplied for the actual PIPULA chain around the maximizer of its \emph{smoothed} marginal. Assume the original finite-valued potential is \(m\)-strongly convex and bounded below; global Lipschitzness of the strongly convex potential is not imposed. For \(\gamma>0\), its full envelope has modulus \(m_\gamma=m/(1+m\gamma)\) and gradient Lipschitz constant \(1/\gamma\). The actual proximal/\allowbreak{}noise recursion equals IPLA for that envelope, and the admissible-step inequality holds automatically. The resulting sharp concentration and Gaussian-chain bound are fully proved. Its conservative discretization term is \((33/20)(1/(\gamma m_\gamma))\sqrt{d_x+d_\theta/N}\sqrt\gamma\), whose coefficient diverges as \(\gamma\downarrow0\). This is not a reproduction of the paper's original-center \(O(\sqrt\gamma)\) convergence rate.



Writing \(m_\gamma=m/(1+m\gamma)\), \(\Pi_{N,\gamma}\) for the full-envelope target and \(\theta_\gamma\) for its marginal maximizer, the exact bound is

\[\left(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_\gamma\|^2\right)^{1/2}\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm_\gamma}}+(1-m_\gamma\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\gamma})+\frac{33}{20}\frac{\gamma^{-1}}{m_\gamma}\sqrt{d_x+d_\theta/N}\sqrt\gamma.\]

Prove full-envelope convexity and differentiability, identify the exact algorithm law, apply the sharp IPLA theorem with gradient constant \(\gamma^{-1}\), and use the already proved admissible-step inequality. This gives every displayed term from the actual chain.




Here the whole joint potential is smoothed, so the relevant modulus is \(m_\eta=m/(1+m\eta)\), rather than \(m\). The score constant of the full envelope is \(1/\eta\). The exact proximal/\allowbreak{}noise update identity reduces PIPULA to IPLA for that envelope, after which the theorem applies the sharp concentration and Gaussian discretization estimates with these constants. The admissible-step proof checks the actual inequality \(\gamma\le2/(m_\eta+1/\eta)\); substituting the unsmoothed modulus would give a different and unjustified step restriction.




\textbf{Correspondence with the displayed declarations.}


\begin{itemize}

\item \texttt{pipula\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}smoothed}: A complete finite-time bound for PIPULA around the maximizer of the smoothed marginal. The original potential may be nonsmooth and need not be globally Lipschitz. All envelope properties, the time-step restriction, normalization, finite transport moments, and equilibrium concentration are proved from the displayed assumptions and the actual Gaussian innovations.
\end{itemize}
